After one of the numerous electrical outrages, appalling in brilliancy and uproar, he said he was sorry he couldn’t have taken her to the old Ornaby farmhouse for shelter; and when Lena reproached him for not having thought of this sooner, he explained too hastily that the house had been struck by lightning and burned to the ground during a thunderstorm earlier in the summer. After that, as she became almost hysterical, he straightway went on to his second blunder. “But nobody was hurt,” he said. “Nobody at all, Lena. There wasn’t anybody in the house; and anyhow I don’t believe the lightning’s really struck right near us during this whole shower. Why, it’s nothin’ at all; I’ve seen storms a thousand times worse than this. Only last summer I got caught out on a little lake, north of here, in a canoe, and pretty near a real tornado came up, with thunder and lightning that would make this little racket today look like something you’d get from a baby’s toy. We didn’t mind it; we just—”

“ ‘We?’ Who?”

“Martha Shelby was with me,” the incautious Dan replied. “Why, you ought to’ve seen how she behaved, Lena! *She* didn’t mind it; she just laughed and kept on paddlin’ like a soldier. I honestly think she enjoyed it. Now, why can’t you—”

===Chinese===
丹把他那身湿透的外上衣裹在她身上，爱抚她，又把湿树枝归拢成堆，说他若能生出火来，一定给她生一堆火。闻言她又哭又笑，笑得可怜。“一分钟热得要烧起来，”她牙齿打战地说，“下一分钟生不着火就得冻死！这叫个什么地方！”

丹自然要替他家乡的气候辩护，可他的道理，同他用湿柴和淋透的火柴生火的种种尝试一样无济于事。莉娜受冷的苦，表现得同她受热的苦一样淋漓尽致；她全然忘了这样的寒暑剧变，在她自己的家乡和别处也并非没有领教过，十足地让自己信了：她的种种苦难，全是“西部”加在她头上的。然而在这一点上，她并不像表面看来那样不讲道理；——凡由内里翻腾而起的苦痛，都精于把自己装扮成外来的折磨。

她的年轻丈夫在给她打气的过程中说了两句不走运的话，叫她这些苦处非但没减轻，反倒雪上加霜。在无数道其光其声都骇人的雷电暴虐之一过后，他说，真遗憾没能带她去奥纳比的老农舍躲避；莉娜责怪他怎么不早些想到这一层，他慌忙解释道，那座房子早在今夏先前的一场雷暴雨里就中了雷击，烧成了一片平地。这之后，见她几乎歇斯底里起来，他紧接着又添了第二桩错处。“不过没伤着人，”他说。“压根儿没有，莉娜。屋里当时没有人；再说，我怎么也不信这一整场阵雨里，雷真在咱们近旁劈下来过。咳，这算个什么呀；我见过比这凶一千倍的暴风雨。就是去年夏天，我在这边北面一个小湖上叫暴雨截住了，坐着独木舟，险些来一场真正的龙卷风，那雷那电，能把今儿这点小响动比成小孩子玩具里弄出来的动静。我们一点儿也不在乎；我们只是——”

“‘我们’？谁呀？”

“玛莎·谢尔比跟我在一起，”丹不留神地答道。“咳，你真该瞧瞧她那时的样子，莉娜！*她*一点儿不在乎；她只是笑着，像个军人似的照划不误。我说实话，她还乐在其中呢。你看，你怎么就不能——”

===Original===
“You hush!” Lena cried.

“But I only—”

“Haven’t I enough to bear? Be quiet!”

He obeyed, gazing out upon the tumultuous landscape, and wondering sadly what made her so angry with him. Then, all at once, beyond and through the mazes of tossing rain he seemed to see, however vaguely, the new Martha he had recognized in that queer night after his homecoming; and the recollection of their strange moment together brought him another not unlike it now. Something mystic operated here; he felt again that same enrichment, charged with an indefinite regret; and though the moment was no more than a moment, passing quickly, it comforted him a little. “There! Don’t worry!” Martha seemed to say to him gently. So he said it to himself and felt in better spirits.

===Chinese===
“你住口！”莉娜喊道。

“可我不过——”

“我要受的还不够多吗？安静点！”

他遵命闭了口，望着外面那片翻腾激荡的景色，黯自纳闷，不知她为什么对他生这样大的气。就在这时，穿过翻卷雨幕的重重迷阵，他仿佛影影绰绰地望见了那个新玛莎——还乡之后那个古怪夜晚他所认出的新玛莎；对两人同处那段奇异时光的追忆，此刻又给他引来一段与它相去不远的时刻。这里头有一种神秘的力量在起作用；他重又感到那同样的丰盈，其中搀着一种说不清的怅惘；虽然此刻无非一瞬，转瞬即逝，却也稍稍安慰了他。“好了！别担心！”玛莎仿佛在他耳边温柔地说。他便把这话对自己说了说，精神也振作了一些。

===Original===
“Oh! Oh! Oh!” Lena wept, huddling in a corner of the shed. “How this horrible old world *does* make us pay for not knowing what to do!” And when he turned to try again to soothe her, she shrank but farther away from him and bade him let her alone.

“But it’ll be all cleared up, half an hour from now,” he said. “You’ll be warm as toast as soon as the sun comes out again, and then we’ll go over the whole Addition and see what’s what, Lena!”

The first half of this prediction was amply fulfilled; Lena was indeed warm soon after the sun reappeared; but they did not inspect the Addition further. They went home, and a few days later Lena wrote an account of the expedition in a letter to her brother George. Not altogether happy when she wrote, she was unable to refrain from a little natural exaggeration.

===Chinese===
“哦！哦！哦！”莉娜哭道，缩在棚子的一角。“这个可怕的老世界，*真是*叫我们为不知道该怎么办付出代价呀！”他转过身想再哄她时，她把身子缩得离他更远了，叫他由她去。

“可再过半个钟头，天就全晴了，”他说。“太阳一出来，你就暖烘烘的跟烤面包片似的，到那时咱们把整个新区走一遍，看看都是些什么光景，莉娜！”

这个预言的前一半充分应验了；太阳重新露面不久，莉娜果然暖和了；可他们没有再去察看新区。他们回了家，几天之后，莉娜把这趟出游的经过写信告诉了她的哥哥乔治。她下笔时的心境并非全然快乐，忍不住添了点人之常情的小小夸张。

===Original===
> You said to me once you’d like to come here to live. Read *Martin Chuzzlewit* again before you do. “*Eden!*” That’s what the famous Ornaby Addition looks like! It isn’t swampy, but that’s all the difference I could see. We drove miles in the heat and choking dust and there wasn’t anything to see when we got there! Just absolutely nothing! People had been digging around in spots and cutting a lot of trees down and after a cyclone and cloudburst that came up while we were there he pointed out a post sticking out of the ground and showed the greatest pride because it had “47th St.” painted on it! This was when we were driving out of the woods. He wanted to poke all over the dreary place, looking at other posts and stumps of trees, but I couldn’t stand any more of it.

> We had the most horrible storm I was ever out in, and it hailed so that after being ill in bed for a week with the ghastly heat, it got so cold I almost died, and then as soon as the cyclone was over it got hot again—it isn’t like ordinary heat; it gets hot with a sticky heaviness I can’t express and the thermometer must stay up over 100 even at night—and as soon as we got home I had to go to bed where I’ve been ever since—hence this pencil—and I’ve just escaped pneumonia! And during the cyclone when I was really ill with the nervous anguish lightning always causes me, he began telling me how wonderfully a former sweetheart of his behaved in a storm on a lake! It was his idea of how to make me not mind it. Of course he only meant to cheer me up—but *really*!

> His father and mother aren’t bad, I must say. They’re quite like him, good-looking and full of kindness; his mother is really sweet and I like them both, though I’ll *never* get used to hearing people talk with this terrible Western accent. To a sensitive ear, it’s actual pain. The brother looks rather like Dan, too; but he’s pompous in a dry way and affected. Reads heavy things and seems to me a cold-hearted sort of prig, though he’s always polite. The father and mother read, too. Their idea is Carlyle and Emerson and Thoreau—*you* know the type of mind—and Harlan (the brother) talks about that Englishman, Shaw, who writes the queer plays. They say they have two theatres open in winter, but of course there’s no music here except something they brag about called the “April Festival,” when there’s a week of imported orchestra and some singing. Pleasant for *me*!—one week in the year!—though I suppose *you’ll* think it’s all I should have.

===Chinese===
> 你从前说过，你想来这里住。你来以前，把《马丁·朱述尔维特》（Martin Chuzzlewit）再读一遍吧。“*伊甸园*！”大名鼎鼎的奥纳比新区就是那副模样！它不算沼泽地，可我看出的分别也就只有这一条。我们在酷热和呛人的尘土里驱车走了好几英里，到了地方却没有东西可看！就是绝对什么都没有！有人东一块西一块地刨了些土，砍掉了很多树；我们待在那儿的时候起来一场旋风加倾盆暴雨，过后他指着从土里戳出来的一根木桩，得意得无以复加，因为桩子上漆着“第四十七街”（“47th St.”）字样！那正是我们开车出林子的时候。他还想在那个凄凉地方满处转悠，看别的木桩和树墩子，可我再受不住了。

> 我们遇上了我生平在外头经历过的最可怕的暴风雨，冰雹砸得那么凶：先前那要命的热已经害我卧床病了一星期，这时又冷得我差点死掉；旋风一过却又热起来——那热不像平常的热；它热得又黏又沉，我没法形容，温度计准是连夜里都停在一百多度（华氏）以上——我们一到家我就只得躺上床，从那时起一直躺到现在——所以才有这枝铅笔——我刚刚逃过一场肺炎的劫！还有，旋风刮着的时候，我正叫闪电——它向来叫我神经上受罪——折磨得真病着，他却开始对我讲，他从前的一个恋人当年在湖上遇暴风雨时表现得多么美妙！这就是他叫我别在乎的高招。当然，他不过想让我开心——可是*真是*的！

> 说句公道话，他的父母人并不坏。他们俩都很像他，模样好看，满怀善意；他母亲确实可亲，两个我都喜欢，只是人们讲话带的这种可怕的西部口音，我*永远*习惯不了。对一只灵敏的耳朵，那是真真切切的疼痛。那位哥哥相貌也颇像丹；可他摆起架子来干巴巴的，还矫揉造作。净读些厚重的大部头，照我看是个心冷、自命不凡的假道学，尽管他倒是一贯彬彬有礼。父母也读书。他们心仪的是卡莱尔（Carlyle）、爱默生（Emerson）和梭罗（Thoreau）——*你*知道那路头脑——哈兰（就是那位哥哥）还谈起那个写怪戏的英国人萧伯纳（Shaw）。他们说这里冬天有两家剧院开演；至于音乐，当然没有，只有一样他们吹嘘的名叫“四月音乐节”（April Festival）的东西，届时有一个星期从外面请来的管弦乐团，再加些演唱。对*我*真是享受！——一年一个星期！——不过我猜，*你*多半会说这也尽够我的了。

===Original===
> They meant to be kind, but they gave me the most fearful “reception.” I never endured such a ghastly ordeal. The weather was over 100 in the shade—and in crowded rooms, well, imagine it! The people were dressed well enough—some of them were rather queer, but so are some at home—but I wish you could have seen the vehicles they drive in and their coachmen! Slouchy darkies in old straw hats with long-tailed horses that get the reins under their tails—and fringed surreys and family carryalls, something like what you’d see out in the country towns in Connecticut. They have phaetons and runabouts and a few respectable traps, but I’ve seen just one good-looking victoria since I came here. They don’t *like* smartness really. I believe they think it’s effeminate!

> The real head of the Oliphant family is an outrageous old hag, Dan’s grandmother, who behaved terribly to me at my only meeting with her—it will remain our only meeting! They’re all afraid of her, and she has a lot of money. Queer—I understand he’s tried to raise money for his Eden all over the town, but never asked the terrible grandmother. She doesn’t believe in it, and I must say she’s right about *that*! Rather!

> How strange that any girl should do what I’ve done—and with my eyes wide open! I did it, and yet I knew he didn’t understand me. I ought to have known that he can *never* understand me, that we don’t speak the same language and never will. I ought to have realized what it means to know that I must live days, weeks, months, *years* with a person who will never understand anything whatever of my real self!

===Chinese===
> 他们本是一番好意，可他们给我的那场“欢迎会”可怕极了。我从来没受过那样一道鬼门关。那天阴凉地里也有一百多度——屋里还挤满了人，唉，你自己想象吧！人们穿着倒还算体面——有些人相当古怪，可家里也有古怪的人——但我真恨不得你能亲眼看看他们坐的都是什么车、赶车的都是什么人！邋里邋遢的黑人车夫，戴着旧草帽，赶着长尾巴的马，缰绳从马尾巴底下耷拉过去——还有带流苏的萨里式游览车、全家出行的卡里奥尔马车（carryall），同你在康涅狄格州（Connecticut）乡下小城看见的差不多。他们也有四轮敞车和轻便小马车，还有几辆体面些的轻便马车，可我来这里以后，像样的双轮轻便马车只见过一辆。他们其实并不*喜欢*漂亮气派的做派。我看他们准认为那是女人气！

> 奥利芬特家真正的一家之主，是个蛮横泼辣的老巫婆——丹的外婆。我同她只见过一面，她对我不客气到了极点——这一面也就将是我们仅有的一面！全家都怕她，她还很有钱。怪就怪在这里——我听说，他为了他那片伊甸园跑遍全城筹款，却从没向那位可怕的外婆开过口。她不信这一套，说句公道话，光*这一件*上她就是对的！千真万确！

> 一个姑娘做出我做的这种事，还是睁大了双眼做的，多么奇怪！我做了，可我明明知道他不懂我。我本该知道，他*永远*不会懂我，我们讲的不是同一种语言，将来也绝不会是。我本该明白，想到自己得跟一个对我的真我永远毫无所解的人，一天天、一星期、一个月、一*年*年地过下去，这意味着什么！

===Original===
> Yet I still care for him, and he is good. He does a thousand little kind things for me that do not help me at all, and the truth is most of them only irritate me. How odd it is that I write to you about not being understood—you who are seldom kind to me and often most unjust! Yet in a way I have always felt that you do understand me a little—perhaps unsympathetically—but at least you give me the luxury of being *partly* understood.

> Yes, I still care for him, but when I think of his awful Ornaby thing I sometimes believe I have married a madman. It is nothing *as I said*—hopeless—a devastated farm—and yet when he speaks of it his eye lights up and he begins to walk about and gesture and talk as if he actually saw houses and streets—and shops—and thousands of people living there! If this isn’t hallucination, I don’t know what hallucination means.

> But since our excursion to the place I’ve almost cured him of talking about it to *me*! I just can’t *stand* it! And what is pleasant, I think he probably goes to talk about it to another woman. Already! A perfectly enormous girl seven or eight feet tall that he’d picked out to be my most intimate friend! Because she’s been *his* most intimate friend, of course. But I suppose all men are like *that*.

> The heat did relax for a day or two—but it’s back again. Sometimes I can’t believe I am actually in this place—apparently for life—and I begin to hope that I’ll wake up. I think even you would pity me sometimes, George.

===Chinese===
> 可我依然爱他，而且他人好。他为我做上千样小小的体贴事，样样对我毫无补益，说实话，其中大半只会叫我恼火。多么奇怪，我竟写信向你诉说没人懂我——你这个待我少有善意、常常最不公道的人！可不知怎么，我一向总觉得你的确懂我一点儿——也许并不同情——但至少你给了我一种奢侈：*部分*地被人懂得。

> 是的，我依然爱他；可一想到他那可怕的奥纳比玩意儿，我有时真相信自己嫁了个疯子。那地方就像*我说的*那样——毫无指望——一块荒芜的农场——可他一讲起它，眼睛就亮起来，开始踱来踱去、比手画脚，仿佛他当真看见了房屋和街道——还有店铺——还有成千上万住在那里的人！这要不是幻觉，我就不知道幻觉是什么意思了。

> 不过打我们到那地方出游了一趟以后，我已经差不多治好了他对着*我*大谈特谈的毛病！我实在*受*不了！还有一件开心事：我想他多半换了另一个女人去谈了。这就开始了！一个十足高大的姑娘，足有七八英尺高，他早就挑定了要她做我最贴心的朋友！因为她一向是*他*最贴心的朋友，自然喽。不过我想，天下的男人多半都是这副*样子*。

> 暑热确实松快了一两天——可又回来了。有时我不能相信自己真的身处这个地方——而且看样子要住上一辈子——我开始盼着自己会一觉醒来。我想，就连你，乔治，有时候也会可怜我的。

## XII / 十二

===Original===
In the minds of Mrs. Savage’s neighbours across the street and of the habitual passersby, that broad plate-glass window where it was her custom to sit for the last hour of every afternoon had come to bear the significance of a glass over a portrait. All long thoroughfares and many of even the shortest have such windows; and the people who repeatedly pass that way will often find the portrait window becoming a part, however slight, of their own lives; but it will seldom be an enduring part, except as a fugitive, pathetic memory. For a time the silent old face is seen framed there every day, or it may be a pale and wistful child looking out gravely upon the noisy world. Then abruptly one day the window is only a window and no more a portrait; the passerby has a moment of wonder whenever he goes by, but presently may have his faintly troubled question answered by a wreath on the door; and afterwards the window that was once a portrait will seem to him a little haunted.

Mrs. Savage’s window had been a portrait so long that even the school children who went homeward that way in the autumn afternoons noticed a vacancy behind the glass and missed her from the frame; but new seasons came and passed, and no wreath appeared upon her door. She had been so thoroughly alive for so many years that the separation of herself from life could not be abrupt, even if she wished it. She did not wish it she told Harlan, one rainy night, as he sat beside her bed after bringing her the news that she was a great-grandmother.

“I suppose it seems funny to you,” she said. “You must wonder why an old woman with nothing to live for would still want to live. I suppose you think it’s because I just want to eat a little more and to lie here listening to that!” With a hand now become the very ghost of a hand, she gestured toward a window where the parted curtains revealed black panes slushed with noisy water by the strong west wind. “How you must wonder!”

===Chinese===
在街对面那些邻居和来往行人的心目中，那扇宽宽的厚玻璃窗——老太太素来的习惯，是每天午后最后一个钟头坐在它后面——已经渐渐有了肖像上那层罩玻璃的意味。凡长街，乃至许多短街，都有这样的窗子；常常打那条道经过的人，往往会发觉那扇肖像之窗成了自己生活的一部分，哪怕何等轻微；但它很少成为经久的一部分，多半只作为一段飘忽的、令人怅惘的记忆留存。有一阵子，那张沉默的老脸天天嵌在窗框里；也可能是个苍白而惹人怜思的孩子，神情庄重地望着外头那个喧闹的世界。后来忽然有一天，窗子只是窗子，不再是肖像了；过路人每回打那儿经过，心头都要怔忡一霎，不多时，门上的一只花圈便会把他那点隐约不安的疑问解答掉；从此，那扇曾经是肖像的窗子，在他眼里便有些闹鬼的意味了。

萨维奇老太太（Mrs. Savage）那扇窗子做了太久的肖像，连秋天下午打那条道回家的小学生们都察觉玻璃后头空落落的，都惦念画框里少了她；可是节令来了又去，她门上始终不见花圈。她活了那么多年，活得那样实实在在，纵然她愿意，她同人生的分离也断不会是突然的。她不愿意——一个落雨的夜里，她这样告诉哈兰（Harlan）；那晚他带来了她当上曾外祖母的消息，坐在她床边。

“我猜这在您看来一定好笑，”她说。“你一定纳闷，一个活着没什么盼头的老太婆，怎么还想活着。我猜你当是因为我还想再多吃几口，还想躺在这儿听那个！”她抬起一只手——如今已是手的一个幽魂了——朝一扇窗子指了指；帘幔分开处，露出几块黑玻璃，强劲的西风卷着哗哗的雨水泼在上面。“你该多么纳闷啊！”

===Original===
“Oh, no,” Harlan said, though she spoke the truth. “I don’t wonder at all, grandma.”

“Yes, you do! How could a young person help wondering about such a thing? Year before last I could still go out for a little walk; last year I could only go for a drive in the afternoons. After that I could still get downstairs and sit by the window; then I couldn’t even do that, and could only hobble around upstairs;—then I couldn’t even get into another room without being helped. And now for a month I’ve not been able to get out of bed—and I’ll never be able to. No wonder you wonder I want to hang on!”

“But I don’t,” he insisted. “I don’t, indeed.”

“You do. What do you think I have to live for?”

“Why, partly for your family, grandma. We’re all devoted to you; and besides you have your memories—I know you have many happy memories.”

===Chinese===
“哦，不，”哈兰说，虽说她说的是实情。“我一点儿也不纳闷，外婆。”

“不，你就是纳闷！这样的事，年轻人怎么能不纳闷？前年我还能出门散一小会儿步；去年我只能下午坐车出去兜兜。再往后，我还能下楼坐在窗前；后来连这个也不行了，只能在楼上一跛一跛地挪动；再往后，没人帮扶，我连另一间屋都进不去。如今有一个月了，我下不了床——往后也永远下不了了。难怪你要纳闷，我干吗还想吊着这条命！”

“可我不纳闷，”他一口咬定。“我真的不纳闷。”

“你就是纳闷。你倒说说，我活着还图什么？”

“呃，一部分是为了您的家里人呀，外婆。我们全家都爱戴您；再说您还有您的回忆——我知道您有许多幸福的回忆。”

===Original===
She laughed feebly, but nevertheless with audible asperity, interrupting his rather stumbling reassurances. “ ‘Happy memories!’ Young people are always talking about ‘happy memories’; and they think old people ‘live in their happy memories.’ I advise you not to look forward to spending your old age in that way! There’s no such thing, young man.”

“No such thing as a happy memory?”

“Not when you’re as old as I am,” she said. “You can only have a happy memory of something when you can look forward to something of the same kind happening again; but I can’t look forward to anything. Yet I still want to hang on!”

Harlan laughed gently. “Then doesn’t that prove you do look forward to something, grandma?”

“No,” she said. “It only proves I still have a little curiosity. I’d like to live twenty years just to prove I’m right about how this baby’s going to turn out.”

===Chinese===
她微微笑了笑，笑得软弱，却听得出一股尖利，把他那磕磕绊绊的宽慰拦腰截断。“‘幸福的回忆！’年轻人开口闭口‘幸福的回忆’，以为老人就‘活在幸福的回忆里’。我劝你趁早别指望老年那么个过法！没有那样的东西，年轻人。”

“幸福的回忆也没有？”

“到了我这把年纪就没有了，”她说。“只有当你还能指望同类的事再来一回，你才算有一件幸福的回忆；可我什么都指望不上了。可我还想吊着这条命！”

哈兰轻轻笑了。“那这不正证明您还是有所指望的么，外婆？”

“不，”她说。“这只能证明我还有一点点好奇心。我倒想再活二十年，就为证明我对这宝宝将来成个什么样儿的看法没有错。”

===Original===
The implication of her tone was grim with conviction—clearly she spoke of a baby who could not turn out well—and Harlan was amused by his own perception of a little drama: his grandmother, clinging with difficulty to one extreme edge of life and prophesying only black doom for this new person who had just crawled up into life over the opposite extreme edge. “I’m sorry you feel so gloomy about that baby, grandma. I’m rather pleased, myself, to be an uncle, and so far I haven’t been worrying about his future. Don’t you think there’s a chance for him?”

“Not with such a mother and father,” the old lady promptly replied. “Dan oughtn’t to have mixed with such a stock as that painted-up little photograph girl.”

Harlan protested a little; coming to Lena’s defense at least in this detail. “But I understand that the particular foible of the McMillan family is the magnificence of their stock, as you call it, grandma. It seems they’re so proud of it they don’t think of much else.”

===Chinese===
她语调里的意思斩钉截铁，透着凶险——分明是说一个绝成不了器的婴儿——哈兰却从自己觉察的一出小小戏剧里得了点趣味：他的外祖母，艰难地攀住生命这一头的最边上，却预言这个刚从另一头的边缘爬进人生来的新人只有黑漆漆的下场。“外婆，您对那宝宝这么灰心，我心里难受。我自己倒挺高兴当上了舅舅，到现在还没替他的前程发过愁。您不觉得他也有指望吗？”

“有那样的一双父母，就没有指望，”老太太立刻答道。“丹（Dan）就不该同那个涂脂抹粉的小照片姑娘那样的门第搅在一起。”

哈兰略微抗辩了几句；至少在这一细节上替莉娜（Lena）辩护。“可我听说，麦克米伦一家的特别癖好，恰恰就是您所谓的门第显赫，外婆。看样子他们得意这个得意得过了头，别的事都不大想了。”

===Original===
“Yes; that’s always a sign a stock’s petered out. When people *put* a lot on what their folks used to do, it always means they haven’t got gimp enough left to do anything themselves. The minute I laid eyes on her picture I knew she came from a no-account stock; and when your mother gave her that reception everybody in town could tell right off what she was. Painted! *That* tells the story!”

Again Harlan protested on behalf of his sister-in-law. “Oh, I shouldn’t make too much of that, grandma. A little rouge now and then—”

“ ‘A little rouge!’ ” the old lady echoed satirically. “She was plastered with it! That doesn’t make any difference though, because a woman that uses it at all is a bad woman and wants the men to know it.”

“Oh, no, no!”

“It’s so,” the old lady cried as fiercely as her enfeebled voice permitted. “It’s the truth, and you’ll live to see I’m right. I don’t want you to forget then that I told you so. You remember it, Harlan.”

===Chinese===
“是啊；那向来是门第败光了的招牌。人们*拿*祖上干过的事*当*了不起的大本钱，那从来就说明，他们自己剩的劲头已经不够干成任何事了。我一眼瞧见她的照片，就知道她出身一门不上数的门第；等你母亲给她接风的那一回，全城的人一眼就看出了她是个什么货色。涂着脂粉！*这就*说明问题了！”

哈兰又替他的嫂子抗辩了一句。“哦，这个不必看得太重吧，外婆。偶尔搽一点胭脂——”

“‘偶尔搽一点胭脂！’老太太讥讽地学了一遍舌。‘她那是糊了满脸！不过这倒不打紧，因为女人只要沾了那玩意儿，就是个坏女人，存心要叫男人们知道她是坏女人。’”

“哦，不，不！”

“就是这样，”老太太嚷道，用她衰弱的声音所能拿出的全部凶劲。“这是实话，你会活着看到我是对的。到那时我不许你忘了是我先说下的。你记住，哈兰。”

===Original===
“Yes, grandma,” he said placatively. “I will if—”

“I don’t want any ‘if’ about it. You remember what I’m telling you! She’s bad!” Mrs. Savage spoke so vehemently that she had to pause and let her quickened breathing become more regular;—then she went on: “Look how she’s treated me. If she’d had the right stuff in her, she’d have been grateful to me for giving her a lesson. If she’d been just a foolish girl who’d made a mistake and painted herself because she wanted to look healthier when she met her new husband’s friends, why, she might have got a little pettish with me for showing her it was a mistake the way I did, but long before now she’d have forgiven me and thanked me for doing it. Not she! That was the last time I set foot out of doors; and has she ever come to see me? She’s never been near me! What’s more, she’s done her best to keep Dan from ever coming here. When he *has* come I know he hasn’t dared to tell her. Do you deny it?”

Harlan shook his head. “No, I’m afraid I can’t, grandma.”

“Do you know why she hates me so?” the old lady demanded. “It’s because she’s bad, and she knows I know it. People never forgive you for knowing they’re bad. And now she’s brought this baby into the world to inherit her badness, and you sit there and wonder I say the child’s bound to turn out wrong.”

“Grandma!” the young man exclaimed, laughing. “I only wonder you don’t take into account the fact that the baby is Dan’s, too. Dan may be a rather foolish sort of person—in fact, I think he is—but surely you’ve never thought him bad.”

===Chinese===
“是的，外婆，”他赔着小心说。“我会记住，只要——”

“我不听什么‘只要’。你把我这话记住！她就是个坏女人！”萨维奇老太太说得火气太旺，不得不停一停，等急促的呼吸匀和些；随后她接着说：“你瞧瞧她是怎么待我的。她骨子里若有一样正气，就该感激我给她上的那一课。她若不过是个办错了事的傻姑娘，不过因为要去见新丈夫的朋友们、想叫自己脸色显得健康些才涂了脂粉，那么，我那样点破她的错处，她兴许会跟我闹一点小性子，可是早在许久以前就该原谅我、谢我了。她才不呢！那一回是我最后一回迈出门槛；她来看过我吗？她连我的边儿也没沾过！不止这样，她还千方百计不叫丹上这儿来。他*就是*来了，我知道他也绝不敢告诉她。你敢否认吗？”

哈兰摇了摇头。“不敢，外婆，我怕我否认不了。”

“你知道她为什么这样恨我？”老太太逼问道。“因为她坏，而她知道我知道她坏。你一旦知道了一个人坏，那个人永远不会饶你。如今她又把这个孩子生到世上来，好承袭她的坏；你还坐在那儿纳闷，我说这孩子注定成不了器。”

“外婆！”年轻人笑着喊了起来。“我只是纳闷，您怎么不把一桩事实算进去：这孩子也是丹的呀。丹这个人也许相当傻——说实话，我也认为他傻——可您总不至于认为他坏吧。”

===Original===
The old lady looked at her grandson querulously. “Don’t be so superior, young man. That’s always been your trouble—you think you’re the only perfect person in the world.” And when he would have protested, defending himself, she checked him sharply and went on: “Never mind! I’m talking about other things now. The trouble with Dan is that he’s never seen anything as it really is and never will—not in all the days of his life! He was that way even when he was a boy. I remember once you hurt his feelings about some poor little brackets he was making with a little Jew boy. He thought the brackets were perfect, and he thought the little Jew boy was perfect, too. When you criticized them both he got into such a spasm of crying he had to go home to bed.”

“Yes,” Harlan said, smiling faintly; “I remember. He was always like that.”

“Yes, and always will be. So he’ll think this child of his is perfect, and it’ll never get any discipline. I’d like to live twenty years just to see the wrack and ruin that’s going to be made by these children born nowadays. Their parents got hardly any discipline at all, and *they* won’t get *any*, so they’ll never know how to respect anything at all. It only takes a little common sense to see from the start how this child’ll turn out. With no discipline or respect for anything, and with such a mother from a petered-out stock, and a father that hasn’t got a practical thought in his head, you can just as well as not expect the child to be in the penitentiary by the time he’s twenty years old!” Then, as Harlan laughed, the old lady uttered a faint sound of laughter herself, not as if admitting that she exaggerated anything, however, but grimly. “You’ll see!”

“You’re right about it this far,” Harlan said. “Dan already thinks the baby’s perfect.”

===Chinese===
老太太愠怼地望着她的外孙。“别那么自视高人一等，年轻人。你的老毛病一向出在这儿——你以为普天之下只有你一个人十全十美。”他正要抗辩自白，她尖利地止住他，接着说：“不用说了！我现在说的是别的事情。丹的毛病是，他从来没有看清过任何事物的本相，往后也永远不会——活一辈子也不会！他小时候就是那样。我记得有一回，你为了他同一个犹太小男孩做的几副可怜的小托架伤了他的心。他觉得托架十全十美，觉得那个犹太小男孩也十全十美。你把两样都数落了一顿，他哭得直抽抽，只好回家上床去了。”

“是的，”哈兰说着，淡淡一笑；“我记得。他一向是那样。”

“是啊，往后也永远那样。所以他会觉得他这个孩子十全十美，孩子也就休想受到一点管教。我倒想再活二十年，就为看看如今生下来的这些孩子往后要闹出个怎样的烂摊子。他们的父母就没受过多少管教，*他们*更是*一点儿*也别想有，所以他们永远学不会敬重任何东西。只消一点点常识，打头就能看出这孩子将来是什么样。没有管教，不敬重任何东西，又推上那样一个从败光了的门第里出来的母亲，一个脑袋里装不出一句正经主意的父亲，你满可以就这样认定：这孩子到二十岁上准蹲进监狱！”哈兰笑了，老太太自己也发出一声微弱的笑，然而那笑不像是承认自己夸大了什么，倒透着冷冷的煞气。“你等着瞧吧！”

“到这一步您说对了，”哈兰说。“丹已经觉得这宝宝十全十美了。”

===Original===
“Happy, is he?”

“The usual triumphant young father. More triumphant than the usual one, I should say. He went whooping over the house till mother had to stop him and send him outdoors to keep him from disturbing Lena.”

“Yes; that’s like him,” the old lady said. “How queer it is; there are people who can always find something to whoop about, no matter what happens. Your grandfather was like that when he was a young man. Even when we were poor as Job’s turkey he’d burst out cackling and laughing over anything at all. I used to just look at him and wonder. Dan’s desperate for money, isn’t he?”

Harlan coughed, frowned, and then looked faintly amused. “Yes, I should just about use the word ‘desperate.’ I think he is.”

===Chinese===
“他乐坏了，是吧？”

“一个照例志得意满的年轻父亲。要我说，比照例的还得意。他欢呼着满屋子乱跑，直到母亲不得不拦住他，把他打发到外头去，免得搅了莉娜。”

“是啊；他就是那号人，”老太太说。“真怪；就有那样的人，不管天塌下来，总能找出由头欢呼一气。你外公年轻那会儿就是那样的人。哪怕我们穷得像约伯的火鸡，他也会为随便什么咯咯地大笑大乐。我从前常常只是望着他纳闷。丹手头缺钱缺得发慌，是不是？”

哈兰咳了一声，皱了皱眉，随后透出些许被逗乐的神气。“是啊，‘发慌’这个词儿差不多正是我要用的。我看他就是。”

===Original===
“He’ll not get any of mine!” Mrs. Savage said. “I’d not be very apt to help him anyhow, after the way his wife’s treated me. He wouldn’t listen to me; he *would* marry her, and he *would* throw all he had away on that miserable old farm! Now I guess he’s got nothing more to throw away.”

“He’s got rather less than nothing now, grandma. The place wouldn’t sell for enough to pay the mortgages, and he hasn’t been able to meet the interest. Father managed to let him have a thousand dollars two months ago, but it didn’t go very far. The truth is, I think Dan’s begun to be a little out of his head over the thing;—he had twenty teams hauling dirt while poor father’s thousand lasted. Now he’s going to lose the place, and I’d think it a fortunate misfortune if I believed he’d learn anything by it; but he won’t.”

“No,” Mrs. Savage agreed gloomily. “He’s like his grandfather, but he hasn’t got a wife to watch over him as his grandfather had. He’ll just be up to some new wastefulness.”

“He already is,” Harlan laughed. “You’re extraordinary, the way you put your finger on things, grandma. He’s already up to a new wastefulness.”

“What is it?”

===Chinese===
“我的钱一个子儿也别想落到他手里！”萨维奇老太太说。“反正凭他老婆待我的那副样子，我也断不会热心帮他。他不听我的；他*偏*要娶她，他*偏*要把自己所有的一切都扔在那个倒霉的老农场上！如今我猜他也没有什么可扔的了。”

“他如今比一无所有还要少些，外婆。那地方卖出来的钱抵不上抵押借款，利息他也一直付不出。两个月前父亲设法挪给他一千美元，可也不顶多大用。说实话，我看丹为这件事已经有点失魂落魄了；趁可怜的父亲那一千美元还没见底，他雇了二十套车马往那儿运土。如今那地方他要保不住了；倘若我相信他能因这一跤学乖，我会把这看作一桩幸运的不幸；可是他学不会。”

“是啊，”萨维奇老太太郁郁地附和。“他像他外公，只是他没有他外公那样的福气，有一位太太看着他。他不过是又要搞出什么新的糟蹋钱的名堂罢了。”

“他已经搞上了，”哈兰笑道。“您真了不得，外婆，一眼就点在筋节上。他已经搞上了一样新的糟蹋名堂了。”

“是什么？”

===Original===
“Horseless carriages,” Harlan informed her. “Automobiles;—‘*les autos*,’ I believe the French call them now. Since old Shelby wouldn’t run a car line out to the farm, and the city council wouldn’t build a street to the city boundary, and the county wouldn’t improve the road, Dan’s got the really magnificent idea that his Ornaby place could be reached by automobiles. He believes if the things could be made cheap enough everybody that’s going to live in Ornaby Addition could own one and go back and forth in it. And besides, he expects to build some horseless omnibuses to run out there from town.”

“He expects to?” Mrs. Savage cried, aghast. “He’s just about to lose everything, yet he expects to manufacture horseless carriages and omnibuses?”

“Oh, yes,” Harlan said easily. “*He* doesn’t know he’s bankrupt! To hear him you’d think he’s just beginning to make his fortune and create great public works.”

“Jehoshaphat!” In a few extremities during her long life Mrs. Savage had sought an outlet for her emotions in this expression; and after using it now she lay silent for some moments; then gave utterance to a dry little gasp of laughter. “I guess it’s a good thing I’ve made a new will! Maybe this girl might have sense enough to clear out.”

===Chinese===
“无马汽车（horseless carriage），”哈兰告诉她。“汽车；——‘*les autos*’，我想法国人如今是这么叫的。老谢尔比（old Shelby）不肯把电车线通到农场，市议会不肯把街道修到城市边界，县里又不肯整修道路，于是丹得了个真正气魄宏大的主意：他那处奥纳比的地产，可以用汽车直达。他相信，只要这玩意儿能造得足够便宜，凡是要住进奥纳比新区（Ornaby Addition）的人都能置上一辆，开着它来回奔波。而且，他还打算造几辆无马公共汽车，从城里往那边开班车。”

“他还打算？”萨维奇老太太骇然嚷道。“他眼看就要倾家荡产了，还打算制造无马汽车和无马公共汽车？”

“哦，是啊，”哈兰轻松地说。“*他*可不知道自己已经破产了！听他的口气，你还当他刚开始发家，正要兴办造福公众的大事业呢。”

“约沙法（Jehoshaphat）！”漫长的一生中，在寥寥几处绝境上，萨维奇老太太都是拿这一声喊给她的感情找出路；这会儿喊过了，她静躺了片刻；随后发出一声干干的小喘笑。“我看哪，我这新遗嘱立得正是时候！兴许这姑娘还有点脑筋，知道抽身走人。”

===Original===
“Lena?” Harlan asked, for his grandmother’s voice was little more than a whisper, as if she spoke to herself; and he was not sure of her words. “Do you mean you think Lena might leave Dan?”

“If he didn’t have any money she might. What did she marry him for? She’s *hated* being married to him, hasn’t she? She must have believed he had money.”

Harlan shook his head. “No,” he said thoughtfully;—“I don’t believe she’s mercenary. I don’t think that’s why she married him.”

“Can’t you use your reason?” the old lady complained petulantly. “Hasn’t she whined and scolded every minute since he brought her here?”

“Oh, it’s not so bad as that, grandma.”

===Chinese===
“莉娜？”哈兰问；外祖母的声音低得不过是一丝耳语，像是自言自语，他没有听真切。“您是说，您想莉娜兴许会离开丹？”

“他要是没钱，她兴许会。她嫁他图什么？这门婚事她*恨*透了，不是吗？她一定是认定他有钱。”

哈兰摇了摇头。“不，”他若有所思地说；“我不信她是贪财的人。她嫁他不是图这个。”

“你就不会动动你的道理？”老太太使着性子埋怨道。“自打他把她带回家，她哪一分钟不是又哭哭啼啼又骂骂咧咧？”

“嗐，还没有糟到那个地步，外婆。”

===Original===
“Your mother says she stays in her room for days at a time.”

“Yes, she gets spells when she’s moody—or at least just quiet,” Harlan admitted. “But she’s not always in them by any means. She’s rather amusing sometimes, and she seems to try to be kind to Dan.”

“Oh, she ‘seems to try?’ ” Mrs. Savage echoed. “*You* seem to try to stand up for her! Do you like her?”

Faced with this abrupt question, Harlan was somewhat disturbed. “Well, possibly not,” he replied honestly, after a moment. “No, I can’t say I do.”

“I thought not. And does she like any of you?”

===Chinese===
“你母亲说，她一待就是好几天，成天关在自己房里。”

“是啊，她有时候犯一阵子郁郁不乐——至少是闷声不响，”哈兰承认。“可她绝非常年到头都那样。她有时候还挺逗趣的，而且看样子她也想着待丹好些。”

“哦，‘看样子想着’？萨维奇老太太学了一遍。*你*倒也‘看样子想着’替她撑腰！你喜欢她吗？”

冷不丁被问到这一句，哈兰有些慌乱。“这个，多半谈不上，”他隔了一会儿老实答道。“不，我说不上喜欢她。”

“我就知道。那她喜欢你家里随便哪一个吗？”

===Original===
“Well, she’s evidently rather fond of mother—and of father, too.”

“Who on earth could help liking *them*?” Mrs. Savage cried, and, in her vehemence, seemed about to rise from her bed. “Do you think *that’s* to her credit? She hates everybody and everything *else* here, and she nags Dan. That means she thought he had money, and she married him for it, and now she’s disappointed. Well, she’ll keep on being disappointed a good while, so far as *my* property is concerned! Then maybe she’ll have sense enough to leave him and give him a chance to get the woman he ought to’ve married in the first place.”

Harlan looked a little startled as his grandmother sank back, panting with exhaustion; the spirit within her was too high and still too passionate for the frail material left to it. The self of her was indeed without age, unaltered, and as dominant as it had ever been, though the instrument through which it communicated, her strengthless body, was almost perished out of any serviceableness. To her grandson there came an odd comparison: it seemed to him that she was like a vigorous person shouting through an almost useless telephone that could make only the tiniest, just perceptible sounds; and he had an odder thought than this: When the telephone was entirely broken and silent would she still be trying to shout through it? She would be shouting somewhere, he felt sure. But what he said, rather sadly, was, “Martha? I suppose you mean Martha Shelby?”

“Of course! Martha could make something out of Dan, and she’s never looked at anybody but him, and she never will. You needn’t expect her to, either, young man.”

===Chinese===
“这个，她分明挺喜欢母亲——也挺喜欢父亲。”

“那样的*好人*，天下有谁不喜欢？”萨维奇老太太嚷道，激动得像要从床上撑起身来。“你以为这就*算她的长处*？这儿除他们俩而外，她见人恨人、见物恨物，还成天数落丹。这说明她当年认定他有钱，为着钱嫁了他，如今失望了。哼，单就*我*这份产业而论，她还够失望好长一阵子呢！到那时候，她兴许才有脑筋离开他，给他一个机会，去娶他打头本该娶的那个女人。”

外祖母力竭喘息着靠回枕上，哈兰微微吃了一惊；她身内的那股魂灵气太高，火气仍然太旺，剩给它的那副残躯已经盛它不下了。她的自我确实不老，未曾改变，依旧同当年一样颐指气使，只是它借以传话的器具——她那软弱无力的身体——几乎朽坏得不堪使用了。一个古怪的比喻浮上孙子心头：在他看来，她像一个中气十足的人，隔着一台几乎作废的电话机喊话，那电话只能送出最细弱、勉强可闻的声音；他还有一个比方这更古怪的念头：等这台电话彻底坏了、哑了，她还照样隔着它喊吗？他确信，她总会在什么地方喊的。可他嘴里说出的却是相当伤感的一句：“玛莎？我想您指的是玛莎·谢尔比（Martha Shelby）吧？”

“那还用说！玛莎能把丹调理成个人物，她的眼睛除了丹没瞧过别人，往后也不会。你也别指望她会，年轻人。”

===Original===
Harlan’s colour heightened at this, and some shadows of sensitiveness about his mouth became quickly more visible. “Oh, no; of course I don’t,” he said quietly.

“She’ll never marry you,” the terrible old lady went on. “I know what you’ve been up to—I’ve had my eyes about me—but you’ll never get her to quit thinking of Dan. And if this painted-up photograph girl takes her baby and goes away some day, things might have a chance to come out right. But *you*, young man—” She stopped, beset by a little cough as feeble as a baby’s, yet enough to check her; and upon this the professional nurse who now took care of her appeared in the doorway and gave Harlan the smiling glance that let him know his call had lasted long enough.

He rose from his chair by the bedside, murmuring the appropriate cheering phrases;—he was sure his grandmother would be stronger the next time he came, and she would soon “get downstairs again,” he said; while she looked up at him with a strange contemplation that he sometimes remembered afterwards; she had so many times in her life said to others what he was saying to her now. But she let him thus ease his departure, and responded with only a faintly gasped, “We’ll hope so,” and “Good night.”

===Chinese===
这话叫哈兰脸上添了血色，嘴边那几缕易感的暗影顿时更显眼了。“哦，不，我当然不指望，”他平静地说。

“她绝不会嫁给你，”可怕的老人家接着说。“你们捣的什么鬼，我心里有数——我的眼睛一向没闲着——可你休想叫她把丹从心里撵出去。哪天这个涂脂抹粉的照片姑娘带着她的孩子走了，事情兴许才有指望理顺。可是*你*呀，年轻人——”她停住了，一阵咳嗽袭来，微弱得像婴儿的咳，却足以把她截住；趁这当口，如今照料她的那位专职护士出现在房门口，朝哈兰投来含笑的一瞥，让他明白这趟探望该收场了。

他从床边的椅子上起身，喃喃说着那些合式的宽心话；他确信外祖母下回他再来时会更硬朗，很快就能“重新下楼”了，他说；她却抬眼望着他，眼神里有一种奇异的沉思，他后来有时还会记起：他这会儿说给她听的，正是她一生中对别人说过多少遍的话。可她由着他这样把离别铺排得体面，只轻轻地喘出一句作答：“但愿如此。”还有一句：“晚安。”

===Original===
Though he bent over her, her voice was almost inaudible against the sound of the rain spitefully hammering the windows; and in the light of the single green-shaded bulb that hung above the table of tonics and medicines at the foot of the bed, the whiteness of her face was almost indistinguishable from the whiteness of the pillow. She was so nearly a ghost, indeed, that as he touched the cold hand in farewell, it seemed to him that if there were ghosts about—his grandfather, for instance—she might almost as easily be communing with them as with the living. She was of their world more than of this wherein she still wished to linger.

Downstairs, the elderly negro who had served her so long waited to open the door for the parting guest.

“You ought to brung you’ papa’s an’ mamma’s carri’ge, Mist’ Hollun,” he said. “You goin’ git mighty wet, umbrella or no umbrella.”

===Chinese===
尽管他俯下身去，她的声音仍几乎听不见，敌不过雨点恶狠狠捶打窗子的声响；床脚那张摆满滋补剂和药品的桌子上方，悬着孤零零一盏绿罩灯泡，在那点光里，她脸色的白同枕头的白几乎分辨不出。她实在近乎一个幽灵了；所以当他握住那只冷手道别时，他觉得，倘若四周真有幽灵出没——比如他的外公——她同他们往还，未必就比同活人往还更费事。与其说她属于这个她还恋恋想多留一时的世界，不如说她早已属于他们的世界。

楼下，伺候了她多年的那位年迈黑人老仆，等着为告辞的客人开门。

“您该坐您爸妈的马车来嘛，哈伦先生，”他说。“这么个下法，甭管带伞不带伞，您都要湿个透。”

===Original===
“No doubt, Nimbus.”

“Yes, suh,” said Nimbus reflectively. “You goin’ swim. How you think you’ grammaw feel tonight?”

“I’m afraid she’s not any stronger. I’m afraid she won’t be here much longer.”

“No, suh?” The thin old man chuckled a little, as if to himself. “She awready *did* be here some few days! She stay li’l’ while yet, Mist’ Hollun.”

“You think so?”

===Chinese===
“那是一定的，尼姆巴斯（Nimbus）。”

“是啊，老爷，”尼姆巴斯沉吟着说。“您这是要游泳去了。您猜您外婆今儿晚上觉着怎么样啦？”

“我怕她不见得好些。我怕她在世的日子不多了。”

“是吗，老爷？”干瘦的老人自己对自己咯咯笑了两声。“她这不是*已经*在这儿待了有一些天了嘛！她还得再待一小阵子呢，哈伦先生。”

“你这样想？”

===Original===
“Yes, suh,” said Nimbus, chuckling again. “Same way as ’tis ’bout anything else. Some people come call on you; stay li’l’ while; git up to go, they walk right out. Some people, they set an’ set an’ set; then when they git up to go, they don’t go; they keep on talk, talk, talk. You grammaw she aw-ways do like that. She goin’ take her time before she walk out the big door.”

“I hope so,” Harlan said, as Nimbus unfastened the old-fashioned brass door-chain for him. “I hope so, indeed.”

“Yes, suh; she take her own time,” the coloured man insisted;—then, opening the door, he stood aside and inclined himself in a bow that obviously gave him a satisfaction more than worth the effort. “I expeck she do you well, Mist’ Hollun.”

“What?” Harlan asked, pausing to unfurl the umbrella he had left just outside. “What did you say, Nimbus?”

===Chinese===
“是啊，老爷，”尼姆巴斯又咯咯笑起来。“什么事儿都是这么个理儿。有的人上您家做客，坐一小会儿，起身要走，抬腿就出了门。有的人呢，坐着坐着坐着，末了起身要走，人却不走，还是一个劲儿地说话、说话、说话。您外婆呀，她一向是后一种。走出那扇大门之前，她还得消磨她自个儿的工夫呢。”

“但愿这样，”哈兰说；尼姆巴斯正替他解开那道老式的黄铜门链。“我真但愿这样。”

“是啊，老爷；她有她自个儿的工夫，”黑人老仆一口咬定；随后开了门，闪在一边，深深躬下身去——这一躬分明叫他称心满意，比所费的力气值当得多。“我猜呐，她会厚待您的，哈伦先生。”

“什么？”哈兰问，停下脚去撑他先前搁在门外的那把伞。“你说什么，尼姆巴斯？”

===Original===
“I mean: What she goin’ do with all that propaty?” Nimbus explained. “Door she goin’ out of when she git ready, it’s a mighty big door, but ’tain’t big enough to tote all that propaty with her—no, suh! I expeck you goin’ git mighty big slice all that propaty, Mist’ Hollun. Goo’ ni’, suh.”

Harlan laughed, bade him good night, and strode forward into the gusty water that drove through the darkness. Outside the gate, as he turned toward home, he laughed again, amused by the old negro’s view of things, but not amused by the things themselves. Harlan knew that he had never won his grandmother’s affection; her thought had always been of his brother and was still of Dan now, as she lay upon the bed from which she would never rise. Whatever the terms of her new will might be, and whatever their actual consequences, she had made it clear that they were at least designed for Dan’s ultimate benefit.

Harlan had little expectation of any immediate benefit to himself, notwithstanding the lively hints of Nimbus; nor were his hopes greater than his expectations. He had no wish to supplant his brother.

===Chinese===
“我是说：她那一大片家产，往后她打算怎么办呐？”尼姆巴斯解释道。“等她预备好了要出去，她走的那扇门是扇大门，可再大，也大不到能把那一大片家产全数背在身上带走——背不动，老爷！我猜呐，那一大片家产里头，有一好大的一片要归了您，哈伦先生。夜安，老爷。”

哈兰笑了，向他道了夜安，迈开大步闯进那阵穿过黑暗横扫过来的急雨里。出了大门，转身朝家走的时候，他又笑了一回——那位老黑人的见识叫他觉着可乐，那见识所讲的世事本身却无可乐之处。哈兰知道，他从没赢得过外祖母的疼爱；她的心思一向在他哥哥身上，如今她躺在那张再也起不来的床上，想的还是丹。她那份新遗嘱条款如何、实际后果又如何且不管，她已把话说明白：那些条款至少是照着丹的最终好处设下的。

尽管尼姆巴斯的暗示活灵活现，哈兰对自己眼下能得着什么好处并不抱多大指望；他的希望也并不比他的期望更大。他丝毫无意顶替他哥哥。

## XIII / 十三

===Original===

He had no wish to supplant his brother in Mrs. Savage’s will or in anything;—last of all did he wish to supplant him in the heart of Martha Shelby. Mrs. Savage had been far from understanding her grandson’s deep pride, and, as he strode homeward in the slashing rain, her acrid warnings that he must not hope for anything from Martha repeated themselves over and over in his mind, as such things will, and upon each repetition stung the more.

He thought ruefully of the ancient popular notion that such stingings come from only the unpleasant truth. “It hurts him because it’s true,” people say, sometimes, as if mere insult must ever fail to rankle, and all accusation not well-founded fall but painlessly upon the righteous. What Harlan recognized as possibly nearest the truth among his grandmother’s unfavourable implications was what hurt him the least. He did not wholly lack the power of self-criticism; and he was able to perceive that the old lady had at least a foundation when she said, “Don’t be so superior, young man. That’s always been your trouble.” Harlan was ready to admit that superiority had always been his trouble.

Not definitely, or in so many words, but nevertheless in fact, he believed himself superior to other people—even to all other people. Thus, when he and his brother were children, and their father took them to Mr. Forepaugh’s circus, Dan was enthusiastic about a giant seven and a half feet high; but Harlan remained cold in the lofty presence. True giants were never less than nine feet tall and this one was “a pretty poor specimen,” he declared, becoming so superior in the matter that Dan fell back upon personalities. “Well, anyhow, he’s taller than *you* are, Harlan.”

===Chinese===

他并无意在萨维奇老太太（Mrs. Savage）的遗嘱里、或在任何事情上取代他兄弟的位置；而最不愿的，才是取代他在玛莎·谢尔比（Martha Shelby）心里的位置。萨维奇老太太远没有懂得她外孙那份深藏的自尊；他顶着瓢泼大雨大步往家走的时候，她那些尖刻的告诫——休想从玛莎那里指望什么——在他心里一遍又一遍地翻腾，这类事情向来如此，而每翻腾一遍，便蛰得愈疼一分。

他悻悻地想起一句古老的流俗之见：这类蛰痛，必定只源于不中听的真实。“他难受，因为那是真的”——人们有时这样说，仿佛单是侮辱便决计扎不疼人，仿佛一切无凭无据的指责，落在正直人身上只会不痛不痒。外祖母种种不利的影射之中，哈兰（Harlan）认作或许最贴近真实的那一条，恰恰伤他最浅。他并非全无自省的能力；他看得出，老太太说“别这么自视甚高，年轻人。这向来是你的毛病”的时候，至少是有几分根据的。哈兰愿意承认，自视甚高从来就是他的毛病。

说得不确定，也不曾见诸言辞，然而事实上，他相信自己是高人一等的——甚至高过一切别的人。于是，他和兄弟还小的时候，父亲带他们去看福尔帕夫马戏团（Mr. Forepaugh’s circus），丹（Dan）对一位七英尺半高的巨人了然神往；哈兰却在这位巍巍人物面前冷冷的。真正的巨人从来矮不了九英尺，而这一个，他宣称，不过是“相当蹩脚的货色”。他在这件事上把高人一等的架子摆得十足，逼得丹只好诉诸人身。“哼，反正他也比*你*高，哈兰。”

===Original===

“I’m not in the business of being a giant, thank you,” Harlan said; and Dan, helplessly baffled by the retort, because he was unable to analyze it, missed the chance to understand a fundamental part of his brother’s character.

Harlan did not go into the giant business, yet he grew up looking down on all giants, since they all failed to reach the somewhat arbitrary nine feet he had set for them. He could not give credit to a struggling giant of seven feet and a half, and admire him for the difficulties overcome in getting to be at least that tall; Harlan really looked down upon such a giant from a height of nine feet.

Yet he was able, at times, to perceive his superiority as an unendearing characteristic and even to look upon it with some philosophic detachment; he did not resent his grandmother’s remarks upon that subject. What he minded was her assumption that he was trying to take Dan’s place in Martha Shelby’s heart; Harlan wanted his own place there, or none.

===Chinese===

“多谢，我可不干当巨人这一行，”哈兰说；丹被这句回敬弄得全然没了辙，因为他分析不透它，便错失了一次理解兄弟性格中一个根本侧面的机会。

哈兰后来并没有投身巨人这一行，却在长大成人之后始终瞧不起一切巨人，因为他们谁也够不上他为他们划定的那条多少有些武断的九英尺线。他不能对一个苦苦挣扎的七英尺半的巨人表一份敬意，不能因为他克服了重重困难、好歹长到这个高度而钦佩他；哈兰俯视这样的巨人，实实在在是站在九英尺的高度上俯视的。

然而不时地，他也能看出这份优越并非一件讨人喜欢的品性，甚至能带着几分达观的超然来看待它；外祖母在这个题目上说的话，他并不记恨。他介意的，是她一口咬定他想要夺取丹在玛莎心里的位置；哈兰要的是在那里有他自己的一席之地，不然就宁可空着。

===Original===

He had wanted it ever since Martha was a handsome romping girl of fourteen and he a fastidious observer a little older. She was a romp, yet her boyish romping never lacked a laughing charm; for, although she was one of those big young girls who seem to grow almost overwhelmingly, she had the fortunate gift of gracefulness; she was somehow able to be large without ever being heavy. And one evening at a “German” for young people of the age that begins to be fretful about a correct definition of the word “children,” she danced lightly to Harlan and unexpectedly “favoured” him; whereupon something profound straightway happened to the boy’s emotions.

No visible manifestations betrayed the change within so self-contained a youth; for here his pride, deep-set even then, was touched;—the lively Martha’s too obvious preference was always for the brother so much more of her own sort. Dan was her fellow-romp, and she would come shouting under the Oliphants’ windows for him as if she were a boy. They were an effervescent pair, and often rough in their horseplay with each other; while Harlan, aloof and cold of eye, would watch them with an inward protest so sharp that it made him ache.

He wanted to make Martha over from a model of his own devising; he wished her to be more dignified, and could not understand her childish love of what to him seemed mere senseless caperings with the boisterous Dan. Yet neither her caperings nor her devotion to Dan was able to disperse Harlan’s feeling for her, which gradually became a kind of customary faint pain. In a little time—a year or two—the caperings ceased; Martha went eastward, as did the brothers, for the acquisition of a polish believed to be richer in that direction; and when she returned she had become dignified, as Harlan wished, but otherwise did not appear to be greatly altered. Certainly her devotion to Dan was the same; and her merely becoming dignified failed to alleviate that customary faint pain of Harlan’s. He still had it, and with it his long mystification;—he had never been able to understand why she cared for Dan.

Harlan’s view of his brother as a rather foolish person might have meant no more than superiority’s tolerant amusement, had that pain and mystification of his not been involved; but, as matters were, Harlan would have been superior indeed if all bitterness had passed him by. He could have submitted, though with a sorrowing perplexity, to Martha’s inability to be in love with him; but what sometimes drove him to utter a burst of stung laughter was the thought that she had given her heart to a man who did not even perceive the gift. To Harlan that seemed to be the supreme foolishness of his foolish brother.

===Chinese===

他想要这样一席之地，还是从玛莎是个十四岁的俊俏爱闹的姑娘、他是个比她略大几岁的挑剔看客的时候起。她是个野丫头，可她那男孩子气的嬉闹从不缺少一种欢快的魅力；因为，虽说她属于那种块头大得几乎咄咄逼人的大姑娘，她却有得天独厚的优雅禀赋；她不知怎么就能高大而不显笨重。一天晚上，在一场专为年轻人办的“日耳曼舞会”（“German”）上——那个年纪的人，已经开始为“孩子”一词的确切定义犯愁了——她轻盈地舞到哈兰跟前，出人意料地“赏光”邀了他；就在那一刻，这少年的心里立时发生了某种深刻的变故。

这么一个自持自敛的少年，内心的变化没有露出任何形迹；因为在这里被触动的，是他那份当时便已扎得极深的自尊——活泼的玛莎那太过明显的偏心，向来落在那个同她脾性相投得多的兄弟身上。丹是她嬉闹的同伙，她会像个小子似的在奥利芬特家的窗底下大喊大叫地来叫他。他们是一对热气腾腾的活宝，彼此胡闹起来常常没轻没重；而哈兰则冷眼旁观，置身事外，心里提出的抗议尖锐得叫他发疼。

他想把玛莎按他自己设计的一副模子改造过来；他盼她更端庄些，又无法理解她那份孩子气：偏偏喜欢同闹哄哄的丹做那些在他看来纯粹是毫无意义的蹦蹦跳跳。然而无论她的蹦蹦跳跳，还是她对丹的倾心，都不能驱散哈兰对她的感情；这感情渐渐成了一种习以为常的隐痛。没多久——一年，或者两年——蹦蹦跳跳停止了；玛莎东行去了，兄弟俩也一样，去修习一种据信在那个方向格外丰饶的教养；等她回来，她果然端庄了，正如哈兰所愿，但除此之外似乎并无多大改变。当然，她对丹的倾心一如既往；她仅仅变得端庄，并没有减轻哈兰那点习以为常的隐痛。隐痛还在，同它一起还在的，是他长年的困惑；——他从来无法理解，她为什么会喜欢丹。

哈兰把兄弟看作一个相当愚蠢的人，倘若牵涉不到他那份隐痛与困惑，这或许不过是优越者的宽容取乐罢了；可实际情况是，倘若一切苦涩都绕开了他，哈兰才算得上真正高人一等呢。玛莎不能爱上他，这一点他本可以认命，虽然带着悲哀的茫然；但有时候逼得他迸出一阵刺痛的冷笑的，是想到她竟把心交给了一个连这份馈赠都觉察不到的男人。在哈兰看来，这便是他那愚蠢兄弟愚蠢之至的地方。

===Original===

Through the rain, as he opened his own gate, he saw in the direction of the house next door a line of faintly glowing oblongs, swept across by wet black silhouettes of tossing foliage; and since these lighted windows at Martha’s were all downstairs, he concluded that she must have callers; for when she was alone she went up to her own room to read, and just before nine o’clock Mr. Shelby put out all the lights of the lower floor. The old gentleman was sensitive about uselessly high gas bills, in spite of the fact that he was, himself, to an almost exclusive extent, the company that produced the gas.

In the vestibule at his own door Harlan furled his umbrella, shook the spray from his waterproof overcoat, and was groping in his waistcoat pocket for the latchkey, when his mother unexpectedly opened the door for him from the inside. “I was standing at a window looking out, and saw you come up the walk,” she explained. “Your mackintosh looks soaking wet; you must be drowned! The doctor was here again awhile ago and says Lena’s doing splendidly, and the nurse just told me she and the baby are both asleep. Come into the library and dry off. Your father’s gone to bed, but he lit the fire for you before he went up. We were afraid you’d be chilled. How did you find mother?”

“About the same, I should say.” Harlan hung his dripping overcoat upon the ponderous walnut hatrack, the base of which was equipped for such emergencies with a pair of iron soup plates in a high state of ornamentation. Then he followed his mother into the library and went to sit by the fire, extending his long legs to its warmth, so that presently the drenched light shoes he wore began to emit a perceptible vapour.

===Chinese===

雨中，他推开自家的院门，望见隔壁那座房子的方向有一排泛着微光的长方形窗子，翻腾的枝叶把湿漉漉的黑影一阵阵扫过窗面；玛莎家这些亮着灯的窗户全在楼下，他便断定她家一定有客；因为她独处的时候总是上楼到自己房里去看书，而快到九点钟，谢尔比先生（Mr. Shelby）就会熄掉楼下所有的灯。老先生对高得毫无必要的煤气账单向来敏感，尽管那家生产煤气的公司，几乎就是他本人。

在自家门前的门廊里，哈兰收拢雨伞，抖掉胶布雨衣上的水珠，正伸手在背心口袋里摸大门钥匙，他母亲忽然从里面替他开了门。“我一直站在窗口朝外望，看见你顺着走道走过来的，”她解释道。“你的雨衣看着全湿透了，你准是淋透了！大夫刚才又来过一趟，说莉娜（Lena）情况好得很，护士方才也告诉我，她和小宝宝都睡着了。到藏书室来，把身上烤干。你爸爸睡下了，上楼前给你把火生好了。我们怕你着凉。外婆那边怎么样？”

“我看还是老样子。”哈兰把滴水的雨衣挂上那座沉甸甸的胡桃木衣帽架——架座上为应付这类紧急情况配着一对雕饰繁复的铁汤盘——然后跟着母亲进了藏书室，在炉火旁边坐下，把两条长腿伸过去取暖，不一会儿，他身上那双湿透的薄底鞋便开始冒出显而易见的水汽。

===Original===

“You ought to have worn your rubbers,” Mrs. Oliphant said reproachfully; and then as he only murmured “Oh, no,” in response, she said in a tone of inquiry: “I suppose you didn’t happen to see anything of Dan?”

“Not very likely! Not much to be seen between here and grandma’s just now except night and water.”

“I suppose so,” she assented. “I thought possibly you might have gone somewhere else after you left mother’s.”

“No.” But there had been something a little perturbed in her voice and he turned to look at her. “Were you at the window on Dan’s account, mother? Are you anxious about him?”

“Not exactly anxious,” she answered. “But—well, I just thought—” She paused.

===Chinese===

“你该穿上套鞋的，”奥利芬特太太（Mrs. Oliphant）嗔怪道；见他只哼了句“哦，不会吧”作答，她便换了探询的口气说：“我猜，你没碰巧看见丹（Dan）吧？”

“那可不大可能！这会儿从这儿到外婆家一路，除了黑夜就是雨水，没什么可看的。”

“我想也是，”她表示同意。“我只是想，你离开我妈家以后，说不定上别处去了呢。”

“没有。”可她话音里带着些许不安，他回过头去看她。“你在窗口张望，是为了丹吗，妈？你在为他担心？”

“说不上是担心，”她答道。“不过——唉，我就是想——”她顿住了。

===Original===

Harlan laughed. “Don’t be worried about it. I’ll sit up for him, if you like. I dare say your surmise is correct.”

“My surmise?” she repeated, a little embarrassed. “What surmise?”

“About how your wandering boy has spent his evening,” Harlan returned lightly. “I haven’t a doubt you’re right, and he’s followed the good old custom.”

Mrs. Oliphant coloured a little. “I don’t know what you mean.”

“Oh, yes, you do!”

===Chinese===

哈兰笑了。“别为这个犯愁。你乐意的话，我就不睡，等他回来。我敢说，你猜的准没错。”

“我猜的？”她重复道，有点不好意思。“猜什么？”

“猜你那个夜游的儿子今晚是怎么打发的，”哈兰轻巧地答道。“你说得一点儿不差，我毫不怀疑，他准是照着那个由来已久的好规矩办的。”

奥利芬特太太微微红了脸。“我不懂你的意思。”

“哦，不，你懂的！”

===Original===

“I don’t,” she protested, with a consciousness of manner that betrayed how well she understood him in spite of her denial. “I don’t, indeed!”

“No?” the amused Harlan said mockingly. “You don’t know that upon the birth of an heir—especially when it’s the first and a boy—it’s always understood by every good citizen of these parts that it’s the proud father’s business to go out and celebrate? Don’t worry, mother: Dan won’t go so far with it that he’ll be unable to get home. Even in his liveliest times at college he always kept his head.”

“I’m not exactly worried,” she explained, with a troubled air. “I know young fathers usually *do* cut up a little like that;—the only time in his life when your father didn’t seem to be quite himself was the night after Dan was born. I’m afraid he was really almost a little tight, and I gave him such a talking to when I was well enough, that he didn’t repeat it when you came along. But I haven’t been worrying so much about Dan’s going downtown and celebrating a little, as you call it—he’s so steady nowadays, and works so hard I don’t think it would be much harm—but I thought—I was a little afraid—I—”

“Afraid of what, mother?”

“Well, he was so exhilarated, so excited about his having a son—he was so much that way before he went out, I was a little afraid that when he added stimulants to the tremendous spirits he was already in, he might do something foolish.”

===Chinese===

“我不懂，”她辩白道，那神气里的心虚却出卖了她——尽管她矢口否认，其实她把他领会得再透不过。“我真的不懂！”

“不懂？”哈兰来兴致了，揶揄道。“你不知道吗，凡是这一带的好公民都心照不宣：添了位继承人——尤其是头一胎、又是个男孩——做骄傲的父亲的本分，就该出门庆祝去？别担心，妈：丹闹不到回不了家的地步。大学里他玩得最疯的时候，头脑也从来不曾糊涂过。”

“我也说不上是担心，”她解释道，神色不安。“我知道年轻爸爸们通常*是*会那么闹一闹的；你爸爸这辈子唯一一回看着不像他自己，就是在丹出生后的那个晚上。我怕他差不多真有几分醉了，等我身体复原，狠狠说了他一顿，往后你出生，他就没再犯。不过，丹进城去、照你的说法小小庆祝一下，这个我倒不很担心——他如今这么稳重，干活又这么辛苦，我想那也算不上多大害处——我只是想——我有点怕——我——”

“怕什么，妈？”

“唉，他那样兴致勃勃，为自己得了个儿子那样兴奋——出门以前就已经是那副样子了，我有点怕，他本来精神头儿就已经高得吓人，再往上面添上酒力，说不定会干出什么傻事来。”

===Original===

“Why, of course he will,” Harlan assured her cheerfully. “But it will only amount to some uproariousness and singing at the club, probably.”

“I know,” she said. “But I’ve been afraid he’d do something that would put him in a foolish position.”

“I shouldn’t have that on my mind if I were you, mother. There’s hardly ever anybody at the club in the evening, and the one or two who’d be there on a night like this certainly wouldn’t be critical! Besides, they’d *expect* a little boisterousness from him, under the circumstances.”

“I know—I know,” she said, but neither her tone nor her expression denoted that his reassurances completely soothed her. On the contrary, her anxiety seemed to increase;—she had remained near the open door leading into the hall, and her attitude was that of one who uneasily awaits an event.

“Mother, why don’t you go to bed? I’ll see that he gets in all right and I won’t let him go near Lena’s room, if that’s what’s bothering you.”

===Chinese===

“嗐，那是一定的，”哈兰乐呵呵地宽慰她。“不过顶多也就是在俱乐部里大呼小叫、唱唱歌儿罢了。”

“我知道，”她说。“可我一直怕他会做出什么事来，把自己弄得很狼狈。”

“我要是你，妈，就不把这个搁在心上。晚上俱乐部里几乎从来没有什么人，像今晚这样的夜里还在那儿的一两个人，断乎不会苛求什么！再说，这种情形之下，他们*巴望*他闹腾一点呢。”

“我知道——我知道，”她说，可无论声气还是神色，都看不出他的宽慰叫她完全安下心来。恰恰相反，她的焦灼似乎有增无减；——她一直待在通向门厅的那扇敞开的门边，那副姿态，像一个忐忑不安地等着什么事情临头的人。

“妈，你怎么还不去睡？他好好进家门的事包在我身上，要是你操心的就是这个，我也不会放他靠近莉娜的房间的。”

===Original===

“It isn’t,” she returned; was silent a moment; then she said abruptly: “Harlan, would you mind going over to Martha’s?”

“What?”

“Would you mind going over there? You could make up some excuse; you could say you wanted to borrow a book or something.”

“Why, it’s after half-past ten,” Harlan said, astonished. “What on earth do you want me to go over there for, as late as this?”

“Well, it’s why I am a little worried,” she explained. “I’d been standing at the window a long while before you came, Harlan; and about half an hour ago I thought I saw Dan and someone else come along the sidewalk and stop at our gate. At any rate two men did stop at the gate.”

===Chinese===

“不是这个，”她应道；静默了片刻；然后她突然说道：“哈兰，你愿不愿意到玛莎家去一趟？”

“什么？”

“你愿不愿意过去一趟？可以编个由头；就说想借本书，或者别的什么。”

“哎呀，十点半都过了，”哈兰说，吃了一惊。“这么晚了，你究竟要我上那边去干什么呀？”

“唉，这正是我有点担心的缘故，”她解释道。“你回来以前，哈兰，我在窗口站了好半天；大约半个钟头以前，我好像看见丹和另外一个人顺着人行道走过来，在我们家门口停住了。反正确确实实有两个男人在家门口停过。”

===Original===

“You recognized Dan?”

“No; it was too dark and raining too hard. I thought at first perhaps it was you with someone you knew and had happened to walk along with. I went to the front door and opened it, but I could only make out that they seemed to be talking and gesturing a good deal, and I thought I recognized your cousin Fred Oliphant’s voice. I waited, with the door open, but they didn’t come in, and pretty soon they went on. I called, ‘Dan! Oh, Dan!’ but the wind was blowing so I don’t suppose they heard me. Then I thought I saw the same two going up the Shelbys’ walk to the front veranda. They must have gone in, because a minute or so afterwards the downstairs windows over there were lighted up. Couldn’t you make some excuse to go over and see if it’s Dan?”

Harlan jumped up from his chair by the fire. “It just *might* be Dan,” he said, frowning. “I don’t think so, but—”

“I’m *so* afraid it is!” Mrs. Oliphant exclaimed. “I don’t like to bother you, and it may be a little awkward for you, going in so late, but you can surely think of some reasonable excuse, if it *isn’t* Dan. If it is, do get him away as quickly as you can; I’d be terribly upset to have him make an exhibition of himself before Martha—she’s always had such a high opinion of him.”

===Chinese===

“你认出是丹了？”

“没有；天太黑，雨又下得太大。我起初还当是你，跟一个认识的人碰巧同路走。我走到前门，把门打开，可我只能看出他们像是说了不少话、打了不少手势，我听那声音，像是你堂兄弟弗雷德·奥利芬特（Fred Oliphant）。我开着门等，他们却没有进来，不大一会儿又走了。我喊了：‘丹！喂，丹！’可风刮得那么响，我想他们没听见。后来我好像又看见那两个人上了谢尔比家的走道，朝前面的游廊去了。他们一定是进屋了，因为一分钟左右，那边楼下的窗子全亮了灯。你就不能编个由头过去一趟，看看是不是丹？”

哈兰从炉边的椅子上霍地站起身。“*没准儿*真是丹，”他说着，皱起眉头。“我不这么想，可是——”

“我*真*怕就是他！”奥利芬特太太叫道。“我不愿意搅扰你，这么晚登门，你那边也许有些难为情，可倘若*不是*丹，你总能想出个体面的由头来。倘若是，千万尽快把他带走；要是让他在玛莎面前出乖露丑，我非难受死不可——她一向对他器重极了。”

===Original===

“Yes, she has!” Harlan interrupted dryly, as he strode out into the hall; and he added: “I don’t suppose Lena’d be *too* pleased!”

“She’d be furious,” his mother lamented in a whisper. She helped him to put on his wet waterproof coat, and continued her whisper. “She’s never been able to like poor Martha, and if she heard he went there tonight when she’s still so sick, she—she—”

“Yes, she would!” Harlan said grimly, finishing the thought for her. “You might as well go to bed now, mother.”

“No, no,” she said. “If it is Dan, I won’t let him see me when you get back, but I just want to know he’s safely in. And try to—try to—”

===Chinese===

“是啊，一向如此！”哈兰干巴巴地插了一句，大步走进门厅；又添上一句：“我猜莉娜也未必会*太*高兴吧！”

“她会气疯的，”他母亲低声悲叹道。她帮他把湿雨衣穿上，仍旧压着嗓门说下去。“她从来就没法喜欢可怜的玛莎，要是让她听说，她还病得这么重，他却今晚上了那儿，她——她——”

“是啊，她会气疯的！”哈兰冷冷地说，替她把话说完了。“你现在还是上床去睡吧，妈。”

“不，不，”她说。“倘若真是丹，等你回来，我不叫他看见我，我只想知道他平安进了门。你想法子——想法子——”

===Original===

“Try to what, mother?” he asked, pausing with the door open.

“Try to explain it a little to Martha. She’s always been such a good friend of his, and he needs friends. Try to keep her from losing her high opinion of him. She’s always—”

“She has indeed!” Harlan returned with a wry smile. “I’ll do what I can.” And he closed the door behind him as gently as he could, against the turbulent wind.

===Chinese===

“想法子干什么，妈？”他问，门开着，人停在那里。

“想法子跟玛莎分说分说。她一向是他顶好的朋友，他需要朋友。想法子别叫她对他失了那份器重。她一向——”

“她确实一向如此！”哈兰苦笑一声答道。“我尽力而为吧。”说罢，他尽量轻手轻脚地迎着外面汹涌的风，把身后的门带上了。

## XIV / 十四

===Original===
Admitted by a coloured housemaid who drowsily said, “Yes’m, she still up,” in response to his inquiry, Harlan had only to step into the Shelbys’ marble-floored “front hall” to dispel his slight doubts concerning the identity of Martha’s callers; his brother was unquestionably one of them.

The heavy doors leading from the hall into the drawing-room sheltering Mr. Shelby’s Corot were closed, but Dan’s voice was audible and although his words were indistinguishable he was evidently in high spirits and holding forth upon some subject that required a great deal of emphatic expounding. Harlan stepped forward to open the doors and go in but halted abruptly, for at this moment Martha made her appearance at the other end of the hall. She came from the rear of the house and carried an oval silver tray whereon gleamed, among delicate napery and china, a silver coffee pot of unusually ample dimensions.

Her serious but untroubled look was upon the tray; then she glanced up, saw Harlan, and in surprise uttered a vague sound of exclamation. He went quickly toward her, but before he reached her she nodded to the housemaid in dismissal. “You can go to bed now, Emma.”

===Chinese===
开门的是个黑人女仆；听了他的问话，她睡眼惺忪地应道：“是的，太太，她还没睡呢。”哈兰一脚踏进谢尔比家那铺着大理石地面的“前厅”，心头关于玛莎家来客的那点小小疑团便告消散——其中毫无疑问有他哥哥一位。

通往前厅与客厅之间的那几扇厚重的门紧闭着——那间客厅里陈放着谢尔比先生那幅柯罗；但丹的语声听得见，虽则一字一句分辨不出来，他却显然兴致极高，正就着什么题目高谈阔论，仿佛非得重重敲打、细细阐明不可。哈兰上前正要开门进去，却蓦地停住——这当儿，玛莎从前厅另一头露面了。她从宅子后部走来，手里托着一只椭圆的银盘，盘中精致的桌布瓷盏之间，一把尺寸格外宽大的银咖啡壶闪闪发亮。

她神色郑重，却不见慌乱，目光落在托盘上；随即她抬眼一望，看见了哈兰，惊讶之间发出一声含糊的轻呼。他快步朝她走去，还没走到，她已朝女仆点了点头，打发她去。“你去睡吧，艾玛（Emma）。”

===Original===
“Yes’m, thank you,” said Emma. “I’m full ready,” she added, as she disappeared.

“I came over because I was afraid you—” Harlan began.

But Martha interrupted him at once. “You needn’t be,” she said. “There’s nothing the matter.”

===Chinese===
“是，太太，谢谢您，”艾玛应道。“我早就想睡了，”她补上一句，人已经不见了。

“我过这边来，是怕你——”哈兰开口道。

玛莎当即截住他。“不必怕，”她说。“什么事也没有。”

===Original===
“I only thought their coming here—disturbing you at this hour—”

“It doesn’t disturb me,” she said. “It isn’t very late.”

“But wouldn’t your father—”

At that Martha laughed. “The chandelier in there fell down one night last winter, and *it* didn’t wake him up! At least I do run the house when he’s asleep. Don’t look so tragic!”

===Chinese===
“我只是想，他们上这儿来——这么晚还搅扰你——”

“搅扰不着，”她说。“也不算太晚。”

“可你父亲难道不会——”

玛莎听到这里笑了。“去年冬天有一天夜里，那屋里的大吊灯整个儿掉了下来，*连它*都没把他闹醒呢！至少他睡着的时候，这个家是由我当的。别摆出这副天塌下来似的神气！”

===Original===
“But I’m afraid they—”

“It’s nothing at all, Harlan. I’d gone upstairs, but not to bed, when the bell rang; and when Emma told me Dan and Fred Oliphant were here, I came down and brought them in and lit the fire for them. They *were* rather damp!”

“But why didn’t you—”

“Send them home? Because Dan wanted to tell me all about the baby.”

===Chinese===
“可是我怕他们——”

“一点儿事也没有，哈兰。门铃响的时候，我已经上了楼，可还没上床；艾玛来报说丹和弗雷德·奥利芬特（Fred Oliphant）到了，我便下楼把他们领进来，还给他们生了火。他们*确实*淋得够湿！”

“可你怎么不——”

“打发他们回家？因为丹要跟我好好讲讲那娃娃的事。”

===Original===
“Good heavens!”

“Not at all!” she said; and as his expression still remained gloomy, she laughed. “Won’t you open the door for me? I made coffee for them because I thought it might do them good—especially your cousin Fred.”

Harlan uttered an exclamation of reproach addressed to himself: “Idiot! To let you stand there holding that heavy tray!” He would have taken it from her, but she objected.

“No; you might spill something. Just open the door for me.”

===Chinese===
“我的天！”

“这有什么大惊小怪的！”她说；见他神色依旧阴沉，她又笑了。“你替我开一下门好不好？我给他们煮了咖啡，想着兴许能让他们舒服些——尤其你那位堂弟弗雷德。”

哈兰发出一声自责的感叹：“糊涂！竟让你站在那儿托着这么沉的托盘！”他想从她手里接过来，她却不肯。

“不行，你会打翻东西的。你只管替我开门就是。”

===Original===
He obeyed, then followed her into the drawing-room and closed the door. Before him, in a damask-covered armchair, was seated his second cousin, Mr. Frederic Oliphant, a young gentleman of considerable pretensions to elegance, especially when he had spent an evening at the club. In fact, since the installation of this club, which the well-to-do of the town had not recognized as a necessary bit of comfort until recently, Fred had formed the habit of arriving home every evening with such a complete set of eighteenth-century manners that there was no little uneasiness about him in his branch of the Oliphant family.

At present he was leaning forward in his chair, a hand politely cupped about his ear to give an appearance of more profound attention to what Dan was saying. The latter stood at the other end of the room, before the fire, and with great earnestness addressed this ardent listener; but Harlan was relieved to see that although his brother’s eyes were extraordinarily bright and his cheeks ruddier than usual, there appeared no other symptoms, except his eloquence, of his dalliance at the club. “No, and always no!” he was protesting as the door opened. “If we lose that, we lose everything! This country—”

But here Fred sprang up to take the tray from Martha. “Permit me! Indeed permit me!” he begged. “It must not be said of an Oliphant that he allowed a lady to perform menial—”

===Chinese===
他依言开了门，随后跟着她走进客厅，把门掩上。迎面只见他的远房堂弟弗雷德里克·奥利芬特（Frederic Oliphant）先生端坐在一把蒙着花缎椅套的扶手椅里——一位颇以风雅自命的年轻绅士，尤其是在俱乐部里消磨过一晚的时候。说实在，自打这家俱乐部成立——城里殷实人家直到近来才承认它是必不可少的一样享受——弗雷德便养成了习惯，每天晚上回家时带回一整套齐全的十八世纪做派；奥利芬特家他这一支的人，为他心里七上八下的着实不少。

这时他正朝椅子前倾着身子，一只手斯文体面地拢在耳后，摆出对丹的议论听得格外入神的样子。后者站在屋子另一头，壁炉跟前，正一腔热忱地向这位热切的听众宣讲；哈兰却宽了心，因为他看出哥哥虽然两眼亮得异样，两颊也比平常红润，可除了一片好口才，他在俱乐部里耽于逸乐的迹象再没有别的。“不，永远不！”门开的时候，他正在慷慨陈词。“丢了那个，我们就丢了一切！这个国家——”

偏在这时，弗雷德一跃而起，要从玛莎手里接过托盘。“容我代劳！千万容我代劳！”他央求道。“决不能叫人议论，说奥利芬特家的人竟坐视一位小姐操持贱役——”

===Original===
“No, no!” She laughed, and evading his assistance, set the tray upon a table. “Do sit down, Fred.”

“Since it is *you* who command it!” he said gallantly and returned to his chair; but on the way perceived the gloomy Harlan and bowed to him. “My dear sir!” he said. “This is an honour as unexpected as it is gracious; an honour not only to our hostess but to—”

“Sit down!” Harlan said brusquely.

“Since it is *you* who command it!” the other returned with the happy air of a man who delivers an entirely novel bit of repartee; then bowed again and complied.

Dan came forward from his place before the fire. “Why, Harlan!” he exclaimed. “I thought you went to spend the evening with grandma.”

===Chinese===
“不，不用！”她笑着避开他的殷勤，把托盘放在一张桌上。“快坐下吧，弗雷德。”

“既是*您*吩咐，敢不从命！”他殷勤地说着，回到椅子上去；半道却望见一脸阴郁的哈兰，便向他一躬身。“我亲爱的先生！”他说。“这份荣幸，既出于意外，又盛情可感；这不单是我们女主人的荣幸，也是——”

“坐下！”哈兰没好气地说。

“既是*您*吩咐，敢不从命！”那位应道，满脸得意，好像刚说出了一句天下崭新的妙答；跟着又一躬身，从命坐下了。

丹从壁炉前面走了过来。“咦，哈兰！”他叫道。“我记得你是去外婆家消磨晚上的。”

===Original===
“I did,” Harlan returned, and added pointedly: “Several hours ago!”

“But it isn’t late, is it?”

“No,” Martha said quickly;—“it isn’t. Won’t you both please sit down and let me give you some coffee?”

“Really—” Harlan began, but she checked him and had her way; though Dan did not sit down. Instead, he returned to the fireplace with the coffee she gave him. “What I was tryin’ to explain to Fred when you came in,” he said;—“it was something I don’t think he understood at all, but I believe *you* would, Martha.”

===Chinese===
“是去来着，”哈兰答道，又意有所指地补上一句：“那是几个钟头以前的事了！”

“可现在不算晚哪，是不是？”

“不晚，”玛莎连忙说，“一点不晚。请你们二位都坐下，让我给你们倒点咖啡好不好？”

“实在——”哈兰刚要推辞，她便把他拦住，依了自己的意思；丹却没有坐下，他接过她递来的咖啡，回到壁炉跟前。“你进来的时候，我正想跟弗雷德讲明白一件事，”他说，“我看他压根儿没听懂，可我相信*你*一定懂，玛莎。”

===Original===
“I beg you; I beg you,” the courtly Frederic interposed. “I was never gifted, yet I understood you perfectly. You said, ‘If we lose that, we lose everything.’ I think you must have been speaking of champagne.”

“No, no,” Dan said, and for a moment appeared to be slightly annoyed; then he brightened. “I told you several times I meant our work for the new generation. The minute a man gets to be a father he belongs to the *old* generation, and the only use he is, it’s to plan for the new one. From then on, that’s what his whole life ought to be—just buildin’ up the world for his son. Now you take this boy o’ mine—”

“Excuse me,” his cousin interrupted earnestly. “You’re referring now to the one who was born late this afternoon?”

===Chinese===
“求你别说下去了；求你别说下去了，”彬彬有礼的弗雷德里克插进话来。“我天分不高，你的话我却听得明明白白。你说：‘丢了那个，我们就丢了一切。’我想你说的准是香槟酒。”

“不，不是，”丹说，一时似乎有点儿恼；跟着又开朗起来。“我跟你说过好几遍了，我说的是我们为新一代做的工作。一个人一旦当了爸爸，他就归进*老*一代了；他仅剩的用处，就是替新的一代作打算。从那时起，他这一辈子就该干这个——为儿子把世界一砖一瓦垒起来。拿我们这个小家伙来说吧——”

“恕我插言，”他堂弟郑重其事地截住他。“您这会儿指的是今天傍晚出生的那一位吗？”

===Original===
“I mean my *boy*!” Dan replied; and his face glowed with the triumphant word. “I have a son! Didn’t you know it?”

“It’s been mentioned, I believe, during the evening,” Frederic answered. “Excuse me, pray.”

“When he grows up,” Dan went on radiantly, “he’s got to find everything better because of the work the old generation’s got to do to make it that way. That’s what we’re put in the world *for*! I never knew what I was for until today. I knew I was meant for *something*; I knew I ought to be makin’ plans and tryin’ to build *up*; but I didn’t see just what for. I thought I did, but I didn’t. That’s what I wanted to explain to Martha, because she’s the only one that could understand. It’s the reason for the universe.”

===Chinese===
“我说的是我*儿子*！”丹答道；这个胜利的字眼叫他满脸放光。“我得了一个儿子！你不知道吗？”

“今晚席间，我记得是提起过的，”弗雷德里克答道。“务请恕罪。”

“等他长大成人，”丹眉飞色舞地接着说，“他睁眼看到的一切都得更好些——老一代就得下力气，把世道弄成那个样子。我们给放到这世上来，*就为的这个*！直到今天我才知道自己是干什么来的。我早知道我*生来总要干点什么*；我早知道我该订计划、该往上建；可究竟为了什么，我看不真切。我自以为看真切了，其实没有。这就是我想讲给玛莎听的，因为唯有她能懂。这可是天地万物的原由。”

===Original===
“You surprise me,” Frederic remarked; and he replaced his cup with careful accuracy upon its saucer on the arm of his chair. “Correct me if I fail to follow you, but are you fair to your son? If he’s the reason for the universe he ought to be able to grasp a few simple truths. You say Martha is the only person who could understand, but have you even tried to make *him* understand?”

Dan laughed happily, in high good humour. “That boy’ll understand soon enough!” he cried. “You wait till he’s old enough for me to drive him out to Ornaby and let him look it over and see where his father fought, bled, and died to build it for him! You wait till he learns to drive an automobile from his father’s and his uncle’s own factory!”

“His uncle’s?” Frederic repeated, turning to Harlan. “Forgive me if I trespass upon private ground, but I haven’t heard—”

===Chinese===
“您可叫我吃惊了，”弗雷德里克说；他把杯子端端正正、分毫不差地放回椅子扶手上的小碟。“我若有领会不到的地方，请您指正——不过，您这样对待令郎公平吗？他既是天地万物的原由，总该领会得了几条简单的真理吧。您说唯有玛莎一个人能懂，可您可曾试过叫*他*明白？”

丹高高兴兴、兴致好极地笑了。“那小子到时候自然懂得够快！”他叫道。“你等着瞧吧，等他长到够岁数，我好驾车带他上奥纳比去，叫他四下里看个遍，看看他父亲是在哪儿出生入死、流血拼命，才把这一片建给他的！你等着瞧吧，等他学开汽车——开的是他父亲和他舅舅自家厂里造的车！”

“舅舅的厂？”弗雷德里克转向哈兰，重复了一遍。“恕我僭越，探问人家的私事——这个我可没听说过——”

===Original===
“I have nothing to do with it,” Harlan said, frowning with an annoyance that had been increasing since his entrance into the room. “He means his wife’s brother.” He leaned toward Martha, who sat looking quietly at the radiant Dan. “Did you ever hear wilder nonsense?” he said in a low voice. “I really suspect he’s a little mad. Do tell us to go home.”

“No, no,” she whispered, and returned her attention instantly to Dan, who was explaining to his cousin.

“My brother-in-law in New York, George McMillan, wrote me he’d got hold of an engineer who’d made designs for a wonderful improvement in automobile engines. McMillan wants to come out here, and he and I think of goin’ into it together. We want to build a factory over on the west edge of Ornaby, where it won’t interfere with the residential section.”

===Chinese===
“这事同我毫不相干，”哈兰说，眉头拧着，那股恼意从他进屋起就一路见涨。“他指的是他内兄。”他朝玛莎俯过身去；玛莎坐在那儿，静静望着满面红光的丹。“你听过比这更疯的昏话吗？”他压低嗓子说。“我实在疑心他有点儿疯了。求你打发我们回家吧。”

“不，不，”她悄声说，随即立刻把心思转回丹身上——他正给他堂弟解释呢。

“我在纽约的内兄乔治·麦克米伦来信说，他物色到了一位工程师，那人画出了一套图样，能把汽车发动机改良得妙极了。麦克米伦想到这边来，我们俩打算合伙干。我们想在奥纳比西缘建一座工厂，那儿搅扰不着住宅区。”

===Original===
“The residential section?” his cousin repeated in a tone of gentle inquiry. “Do I comprehend you? It’s over where you’ve got that tool shed?”

“*No*, sir!” Dan exclaimed triumphantly. “We moved the tool shed this very morning because yesterday the lot it stood on was sold. Yes, sir; Ornaby Addition has begun to exist!”

At this Martha’s quiet attitude altered; she leaned forward and clapped her hands. “Dan! Is it true? Have you sold some lots?”

===Chinese===
“住宅区？”他堂弟用轻轻探询的口气重复道。“我领会得对不对？就是在你那座工具棚那一边？”

“*不*，先生！”丹得意扬扬地嚷道。“工具棚今天一早就挪了窝——昨天，它占的那块地皮卖出去了。一点不假，先生；奥纳比新区已经实有其地了！”

听到这里，玛莎那安安静静的姿态变了；她身子前倾，拍起手来。“丹！是真的吗？你卖出地块去了？”

===Original===
“The first one,” he answered proudly. “The very first lot was sold the day before my son was born!”

“How splendid!” she cried. “And they’ll build on it right away?”

“No; not right away,” he admitted. “That is, not much of a house, so to speak. It was bought by a man that wants to own a small picnic ground of his own, because he’s got a large family; and at first he’s only goin’ to have a sort of shack there. But he *will* build when he sees the other houses goin’ up all around him.”

“Pardon me,” said Frederic Oliphant. “Which other houses are you mentioning now?”

===Chinese===
“头一块，”他自豪地答道。“就在我儿子出生的前一天，头一块地皮卖出去了！”

“好极了！”她叫道。“买主马上就在上头盖房子吗？”

“不，不会马上盖，”他承认。“就是说，起先还盖不起什么像样的房子。买主是个家里人口多的人，想置一块自家的野餐场地；起先他只打算在那儿搭个窝棚之类的东西。可等他看见四周别的房子一幢幢起来，他*准*会盖的。”

“恕我冒昧，”弗雷德里克·奥利芬特说。“您这会儿说的又是哪些别的房子？”

===Original===
“The houses that *will* go up there,” Dan returned promptly. “The houses that’ll be there for my young son to see.”

“Your ‘young son?’ ” Fred repeated. “Your son is still young yet, then? It’s remarkable when you consider he’s the meaning of the universe. You feel that when he grows up he’ll have houses to look at?”

Dan’s chest expanded with the great breath he took; his high colour grew higher, his bright eyes brighter. “Just *think* what he’ll have to look at when he grows up! Why, the nurse let me hold him a few minutes, and I got to thinkin’ about how I’m goin’ to work for him, and then about how this country’s moved ahead every minute since it was begun, goin’ ahead faster and faster till now it just jumps out from under your feet if you stand still a *second*—and it grows so big and it grows so magnificent that when I thought of what sort of a world it’s goin to be for my son, I declare I was almost afraid to look at him; it was like lookin’ at somebody that’s born to be a god!”

===Chinese===
“*将来*要在那儿盖起来的那些房子，”丹应得飞快。“将来会立在那儿、留给我年幼的儿子看的那些房子。”

“你的‘年幼的儿子’？”弗雷德重复道。“这么说，你儿子眼下还年幼着呢？想想他可是宇宙的意义，这就叫人啧啧称奇了。你当真觉得，等他长大，会有房子在那儿给他看吗？”

丹吸进一大口气，胸脯随之挺起；本就红润的面色愈发红了，发亮的眼睛愈发亮了。“你*想想*吧，等他长大，等着他看的东西该有多少！嗐，护士让我抱了他几分钟，我就不由得想，往后我要怎样为他出力；跟着又想，这个国家打从开国以来，时时刻刻都在往前赶，越赶越快，赶到如今，你只要*愣上一秒钟*，它就从你脚底下蹿出去了——它长得这样大，长得这样堂皇，等我想到这将是一个怎样的世界留给我儿子，说实话，我简直不大敢看他；那就像在看一个生来要做神明的人！”

===Original===
He spoke with such honest fervour, and with such belief in what he said, that, for the moment, even his bibulous cousin said nothing, but sat in an emotional silence, staring at him. As for Martha, an edge of tears suddenly showed along her eyelids; but Harlan was not so susceptible. “Dear me!” he said dryly. “After that burst of eloquence don’t you think we’d better be starting for home? At least it would avoid an anticlimax.”

Dan had been so rapt in his moment of vision, his exultant glimpse of a transcendent world for his son’s heritage, that his brother’s dry voice confused him;—he was like a balloonist who unexpectedly finds the earth rising swiftly to meet him. “What?” he said blankly; and then, as secondary perceptions clarified Harlan’s suggestion to him, he laughed. “Why, yes; of course we ought to be goin’; we mustn’t keep Martha up,” he said. “Harlan, you always do find a way to make me look mighty ridiculous. I guess I am, too!”

With that, shaking his head and laughing, he brought his cup and saucer to the tray upon the table beside Martha, and turned to her. “Good night, Martha. I guess I talk like a fool, but you know it doesn’t happen every day, my gettin’ to be a father! I want to bring him over to see you the first time they’ll let him outdoors. I want you to be his godmother, Martha. I want you to help bring him up.” She rose, and he took her hand as he said good night again; and then, going toward the door, he added cheerfully, with a complete unconsciousness that there might be thought something a little odd about such a speech: “What I hope most is, I hope he’ll grow up to be like *you*!”

===Chinese===
他说得这样诚挚热烈，对自己这番话这样深信不疑，以致那当儿连他那位贪杯的堂弟也没有作声，只坐在那儿动情地沉默着，直勾勾地望着他。玛莎呢，眼睑边上忽然沁出一线泪光；哈兰却不像他们那样容易动情。“哎呀呀！”他干巴巴地说。“听了这一阵雄辩，你看我们是不是顶好动身回家了？至少省得再来个虎头蛇尾。”

丹方才正出神在他那一阵灵光里——欢欣鼓舞地瞥见了一个留给他儿子作产业的超凡世界——哥哥这句干巴巴的话搅得他回不过神来；他就像乘热气球的人，猛不丁发觉大地正急急地朝自己迎上来。“什么？”他怔怔地说；随后，第二层的知觉把哈兰的意思给他理清了，他才笑起来。“嗐，是啊，我们自然该走了；不能叫玛莎熬夜，”他说。“哈兰，你总有本事弄得我显得可笑透顶。我看我大概也真是可笑！”

说着，他摇着头，笑着，把杯碟送回玛莎身旁桌上的托盘，转身向她道别。“晚安，玛莎。我猜我这番话说得像个傻瓜，可你知道，我当上爸爸，这可不是天天有的事！等他们头一回放他出门，我要抱他过来看你。我要你做他的教母，玛莎。我要你帮我把他带大。”她站起身来；他握住她的手，又道了一声晚安；随后朝门口走去，欢欢喜喜地又补了一句，全然没想到这样的话在旁人听来也许有点儿不对味儿：“我最盼望的是，盼他长大了像*你*！”

===Original===
Martha’s colour deepened as she met Harlan’s gaze for an instant; and she turned quickly to say good night to the solemn Frederic, who was bowing profoundly before her. “Permit me, indeed,” he murmured, and followed Dan out into the hall.

Thus, for a moment, Martha and Harlan were alone together; and he stepped nearer to her. “Mother wanted me to apologize for him,” he said. “I do hope you’ll—”

“Apologize for him?” she echoed incredulously. “Why? Don’t you suppose I’m glad he wanted to come here?”

===Chinese===
玛莎迎着哈兰的目光对视了一瞬，红晕更深了；她急忙转过身去，向一本正经、正朝她深深鞠躬的弗雷德里克道晚安。“千万容我代劳，”他喃喃说着，跟在丹后面进了前厅。

这一下，屋里有一会儿只剩玛莎同哈兰两个人；他朝她走近一步。“妈要我替他赔个不是，”他说。“我实在希望你——”

“替他赔不是？”她难以置信地跟着念了一遍。“赔什么不是？你当我不高兴他上我这儿来吗？”

===Original===
“But under the circumstances—”

“No,” she said proudly. “I’d always be glad—under any circumstances.”

He looked at her, smiled with a melancholy humour not devoid of some compassion for her, as well as for himself, and assented in a rueful voice, “I suppose so!” But, having turned to go, he paused and asked wistfully: “Are there any circumstances under which anything *I* could do would make you glad?”

“In some ways, why, of course,” she answered with a cordiality that did not hearten him; for he sighed, understanding in what ways he had no power to make her glad.

===Chinese===
“可眼下这样的情形——”

“不，”她骄傲地说。“不论什么情形，我一样高兴。”

他望着她，露出一种抑郁的幽默笑了——那笑里不无怜惜，既为她，也为自己——然后用凄然的声气应道：“我想是吧！”可他转身要走，又停了下来，怅然问道：“那有没有哪一种情形，*我*做的事也能叫你高兴呢？”

“有的方面，嗳，那当然，”她回答得亲切热忱，却并没有叫他振作起来；他叹了口气，明白在哪一些方面，他没有本事叫她高兴。

===Original===
“All right,” he said, and, straightening his drooped shoulders, strode out to join his brother and cousin in the hall.

Young Mr. Frederic Oliphant was lost in a thoughtful silence while the three went down the path to the gate, but as they passed this portal, his attention was caught by external circumstances. “Excuse me if I appear to seek assistance upon a point of natural history,” he said;—“but wasn’t it raining or something when we came in here?” And, being assured that rain had fallen at the time he mentioned, he went on: “That makes it all the more remarkable, my not noticing it’s cleared up until we got all the way out here to the sidewalk. I was thinking about Dan’s speech.”

“Never you mind about my ‘speech.’ ” Dan returned jovially. “You’ll make speeches yourself if you ever have a son. I could make speeches all night long! Want to hear me?”

“Don’t begin till we reach your gate,” Fred said. “I’m going to leave you and Harlan there and go back to the club. But when I spoke of your speech I didn’t mean the one you made over by the fireplace, the one all about your son’s being the meaning of the universe and gods and everything. I meant your last speech—not a speech exactly, but what you said to Martha.”

===Chinese===
“好吧，”他说；随即挺起耷拉下去的肩膀，大步走到前厅，找他哥哥和堂弟去了。

三个人沿着小径往大门走，年轻的弗雷德里克·奥利芬特先生一路沉浸在沉思的静默里；可是经过那道门廊时，外界的情况引动了他的注意。“恕我像是要求教一个博物学上的问题，”他说，“——我们进这座宅子的时候，不是正下着雨，还是在落着什么？”得了保证，说他说的那个时辰确实下过雨，他便接着说：“那就越发稀奇了，我竟没有留意天已经放晴，一直跑到这儿人行道上才看出来。我方才在想丹的那篇演说来着。”

“我的‘演说’就甭放在心上啦，”丹乐呵呵地答道。“你哪天自己也得了儿子，你也要做演说的。我可能做上一整夜！要不要听我做一个？”

“等到了你们家大门口再开始，”弗雷德说。“我到那儿就撇下你和哈兰，回俱乐部去。不过我提起你那篇演说，不是指你在壁炉旁边发的那一篇——什么你儿子是宇宙的意义啦、又是神明啦等等的那一篇。我是说你最后那一篇——说是演说吧也不算演说，就是你对玛莎说的那几句话。”

===Original===
“I didn’t say anything to her except ‘good night.’ ”

“It *seemed* to me you did,” Fred said apologetically. “I may be wrong, but it seemed to me you said something more. Didn’t it seem so to you, Harlan?”

“Yes, it did,” Harlan answered briefly. The group had paused at the Oliphants’ gate, and he opened it, about to pass within.

===Chinese===
“我对她除了‘晚安’，什么也没说呀。”

“我*觉得*你说了，”弗雷德赔着不是说。“我也许错了，可我觉得你多说了那么一句。你不也觉得吗，哈兰？”

“是，我也觉得，”哈兰简短地应道。一行人已在奥利芬特家大门口停住，他推开门，正要跨进去。

===Original===
But his cousin detained him. “Wait a moment, I mean about Dan’s hoping the baby would grow up to look like Martha. Didn’t it strike you—”

Dan laughed. “Oh, that? No; I said something about hoping he’d grow up to be like her: I meant I hoped he’d have her qualities.”

“I see,” young Mr. Oliphant said pensively. “The only reason it struck me as peculiar was I thought that was what the father usually said to the mother.”

Thereupon he lifted his hat politely, bowed and walked away, leaving both of the brothers staring after him.

===Chinese===
他堂弟却把他拦住。“等一等，我是说丹巴望那娃娃长大了模样像玛莎这件事。你不觉得这事透着古怪——”

丹笑了。“哦，那个呀？不；我说的是盼他长大了像她：我的意思是盼他有她那些品性。”

“原来如此，”年轻的奥利芬特先生若有所思地说。“我觉得它古怪，唯一的缘由是：我以为这种话向来是当爸爸的对当妈妈说的。”

说罢，他有礼地举了举帽子，一躬身，走开了，撇下兄弟两个直勾勾地望着他的背影。

## XV / 十五

===Original===

His humour was misplaced, and both of them would have been nothing less than dismayed could they have foreseen in what manner he was destined to misplace it again, and to what damage; for not gossip, nor scandal, nor slander’s very self can leave a trail more ruinous than may a merry bit of drollery misplaced. The occasion of the catastrophe was not immediate, however; it befell a month later, when the Oliphants made a celebration to mark the arrival of the baby and the completed recovery of the baby’s mother. Mrs. Oliphant gave a “family dinner.”

She felt that something in the nature of a mild banquet was called for, and her interpretation of “the family” was a liberal one. Except those within her household, and except her mother, who was still somehow “hanging on,” she had no relatives of her own; but the kinsfolk of her husband were numerous, and she invited them all to meet their new little kinsman.

They were presented to this personage; and then the jubilant father, carrying him high in his arms and shouting, led a lively procession into the dining-room. The baby behaved well, in spite of the noise his father made, and showed no alarm to be held so far aloft in the air, even when he was lifted as high as his bearer’s arms could reach.

===Chinese===

他这句玩笑开得不是地方；倘若两人能够预见，他命中注定还要把玩笑开得更不是地方、闯出怎样的祸，那他们可就不止是惶恐了；因为闲话也好，流言也罢，纵然是诽谤本身，留下的祸根也未必赶得上一句开错了地方的高兴俏皮话。不过这场祸事的引线倒不是当时就点着的；它发生在一个月以后：奥利芬特家办了一场庆贺，庆祝婴儿降生、婴儿的母亲痊愈复原。奥利芬特太太摆了一席“家庭宴会”。

她觉得，总得备下一席近似小宴的场面才像话；而她对“家庭”二字的解释相当宽泛。除了她自己家门里的人，除了她那位不知怎么还“吊着这条命”的母亲，她娘家再没有别的亲戚；丈夫那边的亲眷却不少，她把他们都请了来，见一见这位新添的小亲眷。

众人被引见给这位大人物；随后，欣喜若狂的父亲把他高高抱起，一路吆喝着，率领一支欢腾的队伍进了餐室。小家伙举止得体，并不理会他父亲闹出的动静，被举到半空也毫无惧色，哪怕举得高到他抱着他的人手臂所能及的极限。

===Original===
