“哦，一会儿就过去了，”丹兴冲冲地答道。“不过是那种忽上忽下的毛病——冷一小阵儿，跟着又热一小阵儿。晚安，老伙计。”说罢，他又向这位儿时的朋友道了谢，下楼走进繁忙的街市。

===Original===
After a cloudy day the sky had cleared; a fair sunset was perceptible as a gloomy fire in the heart of the western smoke; and Dan, having long since dismissed his chauffeur, decided to walk home, instead of taking either a trolley car or a taxicab. Before he had gone far, however, he regretted this decision, for his feet had assumed a peculiar independence, and seemed to be unfamiliar parts of him: it was only by concentrating his will upon them that he forced them to continue to be his carriers. “Strange!” he thought. “A man’s own feet behavin’ like that!”

Then he laughed to himself, not grimly, yet somewhat ruefully. Everything he had believed his own seemed to be behaving like that. Ornaby Addition had been as much a part of him as his feet were, but he was making his feet behave; and when he could get his breath, and start in again, he would make Ornaby behave once more. The shellbacks might get Ornaby away from him for a while, but they couldn’t keep it!

When he reached the tall cast-iron Oliphant gateposts, white no longer, but oyster-coloured with the city grime, there was a taxicab waiting in the street before them; and by this time he was so lifelessly tired he wished the cab might carry him into the house, but exerting his will, made his erratic feet serve him that far. He found his brother-in-law in the library with Mrs. Oliphant, who was crying quietly.

George jumped up as Dan came into the room. “Dan, I’m glad you’ve come before I have to go. I’ve got to catch the six-fifteen for New York—”

===Chinese===
阴了一天，天空放晴了；一轮好晚霞依稀可辨，像西天烟霭心头的一团晦暗的火；丹早已打发走了司机，决定步行回家，电车也不坐，出租车也不雇。可是没走出多远，他就后悔这个决定了，因为他那双脚已经摆出一副古怪的独立性，倒像成了他身上陌生的部件：唯有把意志集中在它们上面，他才逼得它们继续替他当脚力。“怪事！”他想。“一个人自己的脚竟这样不听使唤！”

接着他暗自笑了，笑得并不阴沉，却也有几分苦涩。凡是他一向认定属于自己的东西，似乎都在这样不听使唤。奥纳比新区本是他身上的一部分，同他的脚一样；可他正在管教他的脚；等他缓过这口气，重新起头，他也要把奥纳比再管教得服服帖帖。老甲壳们兴许能把奥纳比从他手里夺走一阵子，可他们占不住它！

待他走到奥利芬特家那对高高的铸铁门柱跟前——门柱早已不复雪白，让城市的积垢浸成了牡蛎壳般的灰黄——街边正停着一辆出租车候客；这会儿他已累得没了生气，只盼那辆车能把他一直驮进屋去；可他还是鼓起意志，叫那双不听调度的脚把他送到了那儿。他在藏书室里见到内兄正陪着奥利芬特太太，老太太在悄悄地啜泣。

丹走进屋时，乔治跳起身来。“丹，赶在我非走不可之前你回来了，我真高兴。我得赶六点一刻开往纽约的车——”

===Original===
“No,” Dan said, and he sat heavily in one of the comfortable old easy-chairs. “No. I don’t believe you better leave town just now. They’ve thrown me out of control, but I got ’em to promise they’ll keep you on, George. If there’s somebody there that’s in my interest, maybe when I get on my feet again—” He turned to his mother, looking at her perplexedly: “For heaven’s sake, don’t cry, mother! I’m sorry you’ve heard about it, but don’t you fret: I’ll get back—after I’ve had a few days’ rest, maybe I will. I don’t believe you’d better go to New York just now, George.”

“I’ve got to,” George said. “Dan, I want—I want you to forgive me.”

“For wanting to go to New York?”

“No. For ever introducing you to my sister. Your mother wasn’t at home this afternoon, and at three o’clock Lena left for New York.”

===Chinese===
“不行，”丹说着，沉沉地坐进一张舒适的旧安乐椅。“不行。我看你眼下顶好别离城。他们已经把我从控制权里撵了出来，可我逼得他们答应留你继续做下去，乔治。只要那儿有一个向着我的人，等我重新站稳了脚跟——”他转向母亲，惶惑地望着她：“看老天爷分上，别哭了，妈！叫你听说了这事，我难过；可你别犯愁：我会回来的——歇上几天，兴许就成了。我看你眼下顶好别去纽约，乔治。”

“我非走不可，”乔治说。“丹，我要——我要你饶恕我。”

“因为你要去纽约？”

“不是。是为了当初把你介绍给我妹妹。今儿下午你母亲不在家；三点钟，莉娜动身去纽约了。”

===Original===
“She did?”

“Yes. Your chauffeur took her to the train. She told him—Dan, she told him to say she wouldn’t be back, and she took Henry with her.”

“Wait a minute!” Dan passed his hand over his forehead, and uttered a confused and plaintive sound of laughter. “Just a minute,” he said apologetically. “There’s a good deal kind of seems to’ve hit me all at once. I guess I’ll have to go kind of slow takin’ it in. You say Lena says she isn’t comin’ back home?”

“She had the kindness to tell the chauffeur to say so,” George replied bitterly.

===Chinese===
“她走了？”

“走了。你的司机送她去的车站。她吩咐他——丹，她吩咐他对你说，她不回来了，还把亨利也带走了。”

“等等！”丹用手抹了一把额头，发出一声慌乱而凄然的干笑。“慢着点儿，”他带着歉意说。“一堆事好像一下子全砸到我头上来了。我怕得慢慢儿才能消受。你是说，莉娜说她不回家来了？”

“承她的情，她吩咐司机这么带话来着，”乔治辛酸地答道。

===Original===
“And Henry—”

“Henry went with her.”

“I guess then I better go after him,” Dan said, and he rose; but immediately sank back in his chair. “I don’t know if I’d be able to go on your train, though. I expect maybe I need a good night’s sleep, first. I—”

“Will you leave it to me?” George asked sharply. “Will you just leave it to me?”

===Chinese===
“那亨利——”

“亨利跟她走了。”

“那我顶好还是去追他，”丹说着站起身；可立刻又跌坐回椅子里。“不过我不知道，你那班车我赶不赶得上。我想，也许得先睡上一宿好觉。我——”

“这事交给我办，行不行？”乔治急切地问。“你就交给我一个人办，行不行？”

===Original===
“You mean gettin’ them to come home?”

“ ‘Them!’ ” George said. “I’m not sure that you need my sister here any longer. I don’t think you ever needed her very much. But you do want your son, and if you’ll leave it to me, I think I can bring him. Will you, Dan?”

“I guess I’ll have to—just now,” Dan answered, with a repetition of his apologetic laugh. “It’s all seemed to’ve kind of hit me at once, as it were, George. I’m afraid what I need’s a good night’s sleep. I’m afraid I’ll have to leave it to you.”

“I’ll bring him!” McMillan promised. “I’ll have him back here with me four days from now.”

===Chinese===
“你是说，叫他们回家来？”

“‘他们’！”乔治说。“我可说不准，你还需要我妹妹在这儿待下去。我从来也不觉得你多么需要过她。可你的儿子，你是当真要的；只要你交给我办，我想我能把他带回来。交给我，行吗，丹？”

“我看我也只好——眼下只好如此，”丹答道，又发出一声歉然的干笑。“这一切仿佛一下子全砸到我头上来了，可以这么说，乔治。我怕我眼下需要的，就是睡上一宿好觉。我怕只好交给你了。”

“我一定把他带回来！”麦克米伦允诺道。“四天以后，他就回到这儿，同我在一起。”

## XXX / 三十

===Original===
He made this promise with an angrily confident determination to fulfil it, but the next few days were to teach him that he had not yet learned all there was to know about his sister. When he forced his way to an interview with her in her rooms in the hotel to which she had gone in New York, she laughed at his fury.

“*Why* haven’t I been a good wife to him?” she asked. “I’ve spent quite a number of years in purgatory, trying to stick to what I undertook when he married me! Oh, yes, I know you like the place, George; and I don’t challenge your viewpoint. But I have my own, and, whether it’s right or not, it’s mine and I can’t get rid of it. I suffer by it, and I have to live by it—and to me the place has always been a purgatory. It’s interesting to you, but it’s hideous to me. You like the people;—to you they seem intelligent and friendly. To me they’re intrusive barbarians with unbearable voices. I stood it at first because I had to; I didn’t have anywhere else to go, and I did care for Dan. Then I kept on standing it because I’d got the habit, I suppose, and because it’s hard to get the courage to break away. Well, thank Heaven, something’s given me the courage at last. I was always just on the very verge of it, and the trouble about Henry pushed me over. I’ve perished for years because I couldn’t get a breath of art; I haven’t lived—”

“You could have!” he cried. “With such a man—”

“Dan? Good heavens! I might go on living with a man, even after I’d stopped caring for him, if he still cared for me; but it’s years since I realized absolutely that neither of us cared for the other. I knew then I’d have to do this some day.”

===Chinese===
他许下这个诺言时，怀着满腔怒气、信心十足的决心要去履行它；然而接着的几天却让他明白，关于他这位妹妹，他还有许多不曾了解的地方。他硬闯到她在纽约所住那家旅馆里的房间去见她，她却对着他的怒火笑了起来。

“*为什么*我就不是他的好妻子？”她问。“这些年我过的简直是炼狱的日子，拼着命恪守我嫁他时应下的本分！哦，是啊，我知道你喜欢那地方，乔治；我也不驳你的看法。可我有我自己的看法，它对不对姑且不论，它是我的，我甩不掉它。我为它受苦，又只能靠它过活——对我来说，那地方从来就是一座炼狱。你觉得它有意思，我却觉得它丑恶不堪。你喜欢那些人——在你眼里，他们又聪明又和气。在我看来，他们是一群闯进来的野蛮人，嗓音叫人忍无可忍。起初我忍着，是因为不得不忍；我没有别处可去，而且我确实是爱丹的。后来一直忍下去，我想是成了习惯，也因为没有勇气挣脱。好了，谢天谢地，到底有什么给了我这份勇气。我一向就悬在这份勇气的边缘上，小亨利的麻烦事把我推了过去。这些年来我一直枯萎着，因为我吸不到一口艺术的气息；我没有活过——”

“你有的是机会！”他嚷道。“跟这样一个人——”

“丹？我的天！要是一个人还在乎我，就算我不再在乎他，我也可以照样跟他过下去；可是好多年前我就彻底明白了，我们两个谁也不在乎谁。从那时起我就知道，早晚有一天我得走这一步。”

===Original===
“And how beautifully you did do it!” her brother exclaimed. “His mother told me about your screaming and storming at Dan after he brought that miserable boy home. Do you think I didn’t understand? You *wanted* a quarrel to justify your going, so that the real reason wouldn’t be suspected. You’d seen that singing *beef* again, and you meant to see him *again*—oh, I kept near you that night, and I read you, every instant! You haven’t fooled me about what gave you the ‘courage,’ Lena! It was indeed ‘the breath of art,’ old girl, and not ‘the trouble about Henry!’ You made that quarrel with Dan deliberately. It was to cover what you weren’t thoroughbred enough to face. You weren’t honest enough to—”

“At least I’m honest enough to tell you that you’re wasting your breath,” Lena said coolly. “You want to take Henry home with you, but he doesn’t care to go. He behaved idiotically there—it *isn’t* a good place for him—and of course, under the circumstances, he’s embarrassed about going back. He wants to stay with me just now, and he’ll do what I tell him. You can’t take him back with you, but if you’ll obtain a proper allowance for me, or a settlement, from my husband, I’ll arrange later for Henry to spend a part of his time with his father. That’s absolutely the best I’ll do, and you’d better run back and make it quite clear to Dan. I bear him no ill will, and I’ll be perfectly fair with him on the terms I’ve just mentioned.”

Her brother’s bitterness with her was not abated; but to effect his purpose he tried more reasonable persuasions, and when these were unavailing, raged again. All he did was useless; he could neither shake her nor exert the slightest influence upon Henry, though he continued the siege for three days over the four that he had promised. Then he returned, a defeated but fuming negotiator, to report his failure. His final instructions from his sister were to make it quite clear to Dan that she bore him no ill will and wished him well.

But when George reached the old house of the Oliphants, driving there directly from the train, he was told that he could not make her message clear to her husband; that he could not make anything clear to him.

===Chinese===
“你干得可真漂亮！”她的哥哥嚷道。“他把那个可怜的孩子带回家以后，你冲着丹又喊又叫地大闹了一场，他母亲都告诉我了。你以为我没看明白？你是*存心*要吵一场，好给自己出走找个名正言顺的由头，叫人疑心不到真正的原因上去。你见过那块会唱歌的*牛肉*了，你还打算*再*见他——哼，那天晚上我一直在你身边守着，你的心思我一刻也没看错过！你那点‘勇气’是打哪儿来的，可糊弄不了我，莉娜！那确实是‘艺术的气息’，老姑娘，不是‘小亨利的麻烦事’！你是存心跟丹吵那一场的。就为了遮掩你没那份名门气度去直面的事。你还没诚实到那个地步——”

“至少我够诚实，可以告诉你：你在白费口舌，”莉娜冷冷地说。“你想把小亨利带回家去，可他不愿意走。他在那儿干的事蠢透了——那地方对他*不是*个好地方——当然喽，闹到这个地步，他也不好意思回去。他眼下想跟我待在一起，他会照我吩咐的去做。你带不走他；可你要是能从我丈夫那里给我弄到一份像样的津贴，或者一笔了断的安置费，往后我会安排小亨利拿出一些时间陪他父亲。我顶多做到这一步，你最好赶紧回去，把这话跟丹说个一清二楚。我对他并无恶意，照我刚才说的条件，我会跟他把账算得公公平平。”

她哥哥对她的怨气并没有平下去；可是为了达到目的，他又试着换上比较讲道理的劝说，这些不见效，便又发起怒来。他做的一切都是白费；他既动摇不了她，对小亨利也使不上一丝一毫的影响，尽管他在许下的四天之外又围攻了三天。随后他回来了——一个吃了败仗却满肚子火的谈判人——回来报告他失败了。妹妹给他的最后训令是：要跟丹把话说得清清楚楚，她对他并无恶意，还祝他好。

可是乔治从火车站直接驱车赶到奥利芬特家的老宅时，却被告知：她那句口信，他没法向她丈夫说清楚；什么事他都没法跟他说清楚。

===Original===
Harlan took the dismayed traveller into the library. “The doctor says the trouble is there isn’t anything to build up a resistance,” Harlan said. “You see Dan’s never taken any care of his health—‘too busy,’ of course—and he’s exhausted his vitality. He caught a fearful cold going round in the rain hunting for that precious boy of his, and instead of staying in bed and nursing himself, he was hustling all over the place in a drizzle the next morning. He was all run-down to start with, and his system couldn’t afford it. At least, that’s what they told us after the consultation yesterday afternoon.”

“Consultation?” McMillan repeated blankly, though Harlan’s manner had already prepared him for words worse than this.

Harlan sighed audibly, and shook his head. “Both lungs are congested, they told us early this morning. He can’t—” He went to the bay window and looked down at the slightly frayed upholstery of the easy-chair it had once been his wont to occupy there. “Well, at your age and mine we’ve had experience of sickness enough to know that nobody can stand *that* long.”

“Yes,” McMillan groaned. “I suppose so.”

“I think we won’t tell him you’ve got back,” Harlan said. “He’s asked about it every now and then—wants to know if you’ve brought Henry yet. It’ll be better to let him keep on expecting him than to tell him you’ve come back alone. I telegraphed you after the consultation, but by that time you’d already left New York, of course.”

===Chinese===
哈兰把这位惊慌失措的旅人领进藏书室。“医生说，麻烦就麻烦在身上没有一点底子，抵不住病，”哈兰说。“你知道，丹从来不肯保重身体——当然，总是‘太忙’——他的元气已经耗光了。那天为了找他那个宝贝儿子，他冒着雨满城转，着了可怕的重感冒；本该躺在床上将养，第二天早晨却又冒着蒙蒙细雨满处奔走。他起先就已经垮得差不多了，身体再也经不起这一下。至少，昨天下午会诊之后，他们是这么跟我们说的。”

“会诊？”麦克米伦怔怔地重复道，虽然哈兰的神气早已让他有了准备，预备听到比这更坏的话。

哈兰出声叹了口气，摇了摇头。“今天一早他们告诉我们，两肺都充血了。他熬不——”他走到凸窗前，低头看了看窗边那张安乐椅上已经有些磨旧的椅面，他从前惯于坐在那里。“唉，像你我这个年纪，病痛见得也够多了，总该知道谁也撑不了*那么*久的。”

“是啊，”麦克米伦呻吟似的说。“我想是吧。”

“我想，咱们先别告诉他你回来了，”哈兰说。“他时不时就要问起——问你把小亨利带来了没有。让他接着盼着，总比告诉他你一个人回来要好。会诊之后我给你拍了电报，不过那时候你自然已经离开纽约了。”

===Original===
“Yes; it didn’t reach me.”

Then, for a time, neither of them found more to say. Harlan, near the window, stared out into the smoke haze that a cloudy day held down upon the city; McMillan sat frowning at the floor, and the room was vaguely noisy with a confusion of sounds from outdoors: hammerings and clatterings of steel where buildings were going up; the rending of timbers and crashes and shoutings where they were going down; the uproar of ponderous trucks grinding by upon the brick-paved cross street to the south, so that the strong old house trembled with the subterranean communication of their vibrations—all to the incessantly rasped accompaniment of motor signals on the avenue.

“Isn’t this a hell to be sick in?” Harlan asked, turning abruptly to McMillan. “We couldn’t raise the windows to give him air without giving him this infernal smoke that makes him cough harder. And the noise—there’s hardly a respite from it all night long! When the workmen go home the joy-riders and the taxis keep it up till daylight. He was too sick to be taken to a hospital or—” He interrupted himself with a desperate laugh. “We almost had to! Yesterday morning the servants called me, and I found the house full of men; they’d brought trucks right across the lawn, and started to work. They’d come to wreck the house—to tear it down. I told the foreman my brother was very sick, and he said in that case we’d better take him to a hospital; he had his orders from the contractor, and he was going ahead! Some of his men were already on the roof, making a horrible noise and tearing away the slate—throwing it down into the yard under Dan’s window. I had hard work to get rid of them; and they left a great hole in the roof when they went. My heaven! when such things happen how’s anybody ever to see any *meaning* in life?”

“I don’t know!” George groaned. “I don’t see much meaning in anything—not after what you’ve told me about Dan’s condition.”

===Chinese===
“是啊；电报没有送到我手上。”

随后有一阵子，两个人都找不出更多的话来说。哈兰站在窗边，凝望着阴天压在这座城上头的烟雾；麦克米伦坐在那里对着地板皱眉头，屋子里隐隐约约满是户外嘈杂的声浪，乱作一团：起楼的地方，是钢铁的锤打和铿锵；拆楼的地方，是木料的撕裂、塌落的轰响和工人的喊叫；南边那条铺砖的横街上，沉重的货车碾过时隆隆不绝，这座坚固的老宅也随着地底下传来的震颤瑟瑟发抖——而这一切，都伴着大道上汽车鸣号那一阵接一阵永不休止的刺耳聒噪。

“人病倒在这么个地方，岂不是活受罪？”哈兰猛地转向麦克米伦问道。“想把窗子支起来给他透口气都不行，一开窗就是这股遭瘟的烟，呛得他咳得更凶。还有这噪音——一整夜几乎没有片刻消停！做工的下了班，兜风客和出租汽车又吵到天亮。他病得太重，没法送医院，要不然——”他惨然一笑，把话截住了。“我们差一点就只好送了！昨天早晨佣人们来叫我，我下楼一看，满屋子都是人；卡车一直开过草坪，已经动起手来。他们是来拆这房子的——来把它拆平。我告诉工头我哥哥病得很重，他说那么一来顶好把病人送医院；他拿着承包商的命令，他照样要干！他手下一些人已经上了房顶，闹出可怕的动静，撬石板瓦——往丹窗底下的院子里扔。我好容易才把他们打发走；他们走的时候，房顶上留下一个大窟窿。我的老天！出了这样的事，人还上哪儿去看得出生活有什么*意义*？”

“我不知道！”乔治呻吟道。“什么事我也看不出多少意义来——听了你讲的丹的病情，就更看不出了。”

===Original===
“McMillan, I don’t see a bit of meaning to the whole miserable business. Here’s my brother spent all his days and nights—and all his strength and health—just blindly building up a bigger confusion and uproar that smashes him; and then when he *is* smashed, it keeps on bothering him and disturbing him—yes, and choking him!—on his very deathbed! I know your theory that it all means power, and that power may be thought beautiful—but it can’t last, because nothing can last. So what the deuce is the good of it?”

And when the other, groaning again, said that he didn’t know, Harlan groaned, too—then crossed the room to where George sat in a crumpled attitude, touched him lightly on the shoulder, and turned away. “You’re a good fellow, McMillan, and you haven’t anything in the world to reproach yourself with. I don’t think he’s minded Lena’s going away; he hasn’t spoken of her at all, and I really believe he doesn’t think of her. Your record with Dan is all right, but I’ve been realizing that mine isn’t. I could have made success easier for him long ago; though I don’t reproach myself so much with that, because he *did* get his success—for a while, and that’s all anybody gets—and he enjoyed it all the more for having got it without help. What I’m thinking about this morning: I seem to have spent a great part of my life saying, ‘What’s the good of it?’ as I did just now, and it’s my brother’s work I’ve been saying it about. I’ve always been ‘superior’—and I’ll never be different. I was born so, I believe, and didn’t see it in time. The most I’ve ever actually *done* was to help organize a dilettante musical club! And Dan—well, I hope it’s as you intimated the other night on Martha’s porch—I hope Dan’s been too busy to be much bothered about my ‘judgments!’ I’ve been just nothing; but even if he falls, he’s at least been a branch of the growing tree, though we don’t know where it’s growing to, or why.”

“No,” McMillan said. “We don’t know anything.”

Harlan had begun to pace up and down the room. “I didn’t understand that Dan was in real trouble financially,” he said. “He’d been on the edge so often—I talked about it, but I’d got to thinking of it as a permanent thing for him to be on the edge. I didn’t realize he might actually fall *off*—not until that little Jew friend of his came to me the other morning and *made* me realize it. Well, there’s one thing I can be thankful for: I can be grateful that all I thought of, for once in my life, was that I was Dan’s brother!”

===Chinese===
“麦克米伦，这一整场凄凄惨惨的把戏，我看不出半点意义。我哥哥把所有的日夜——把全部的力气和健康——都搭了进去，不过是在盲目地垒起一场更大的混乱和喧嚣，好把自己砸个粉碎；等他*当真*被砸碎了，那东西还缠着他不放，搅得他不得安宁——是啊，还扼住他的喉咙！——就在他弥留的病榻上！我知道你的理论，说这一切意味着力量，说力量也可以看成美——可它长不了，因为没有什么是长不了的。那么这鬼东西到底顶个什么用？”

对方又呻吟了一声，说他不知道；哈兰也呻吟了一声——随后穿过屋子，走到乔治蜷坐着的地方，在他肩上轻轻一按，又转过身去。“你是个好人，麦克米伦，这世上没有什么事值得你自责。我看他并没有把莉娜走的事放在心上；他压根儿没提起过她，我真相信他根本不想她。你在丹跟前是问心无愧的，我却在渐渐看清，我并不然。我早就可以让他成功得容易一些；这一层我倒不怎样自责，因为他*到底*还是成功了——哪怕只是一阵子，而任何人所能得到的也不过是一阵子——何况他这份成功全靠自己挣来，享用起来也就加倍地受用。今天早晨我心里盘算的是：我这一辈子，好像有很大一部分都花在一句话上——‘这有什么用？’——方才我又说了一遍，而我一直在对着我哥哥的事业说这句话。我从来都‘高人一等’——而且永远改不掉了。我相信我生来如此，只是没能及时看清。我实实*做过*的事里头，顶大的不过是帮着张罗起一个玩票的音乐俱乐部！至于丹——唉，但愿就像那天晚上你在玛莎家游廊上暗示的那样——但愿丹忙得顾不上把我的‘评判’太当回事！我一直算不得什么；可是他即便倒下，至少也算是那棵生长中的树上的一根枝条，虽然我们不知道这树要长到哪里去，也不知道它为什么要长。”

“是啊，”麦克米伦说。“我们什么都不知道。”

哈兰已经在屋里踱来踱去。“我从前不明白丹在钱财上真的陷了困境，”他说。“他悬在崖边那么多次——这事我也议论过，可是议论来议论去，竟把悬在崖边当成了他长年如此的光景。我没想过他真会*跌下去*——直到前几天早晨，他那个小个子犹太朋友找上门来，*逼*着我看清了这一点。好吧，有一桩事我总可以感谢上天：我这一生里总算有一回，满心想的只是我是丹的弟弟！”

===Original===
“Harlan?” Martha Shelby’s voice called him softly from the stairway.

“Yes?” He turned to the door, explaining, “Dan may want me—he sends for me to come in sometimes. Perhaps you might—” He paused.

“Yes,” George said, rising. “I’ll go and wire her. She might want to come. At any rate she’ll send Henry. Then I’ll come back here. I’ll be downstairs in this room, if there’s anything—”

“I’ll let you know,” Harlan said, and he went upstairs to Martha.

===Chinese===
“哈兰？”楼梯口传来玛莎·谢尔比轻声的呼唤。

“什么事？”他朝门口转过身，解释道：“丹也许要找我——他有时候就打发人叫我进去。也许你可以——”他顿住了。

“好，”乔治说着站起身。“我去给她拍电报。她也许想回来。无论如何她会把小亨利打发来。然后我再回到这儿来。要有什么事，我就在楼下这间屋里——”

“我会叫人告诉你，”哈兰说着上楼找玛莎去了。

===Original===
“Your mother’s been with him,” she whispered. “She and the nurse said he seemed to be trying to ask for somebody, but he was so weak, and his cough troubled him so much—”

“I’ll go in and see,” he said; but he came back to her a few moments later, and told her it was for her that Dan was asking.

She went into his room, sat by his bed, and put her hand gently over his on the coverlet. “Why, you’re better, Dan,” she said, as he turned his head and looked at her with eyes that cleared and grew brighter, for he recognized her.

“Think so?” He spoke distinctly though his voice was weak. “Well, maybe—maybe. I did hope—”

===Chinese===
“你母亲一直守在他跟前，”她低声说。“她和护士说，他好像想要问什么人，可是他太虚弱了，咳嗽又折腾得他厉害——”

“我进去看看，”他说；可是过了片刻他又回到她跟前，告诉她，丹要找的是她。

她走进他的房间，在床边坐下，把自己的手轻轻覆在他放在床罩上的手上。“咦，你好些了，丹，”她说。他转过头来看她，眼神清亮起来，也亮了起来，因为他认出了她。

“你这么觉得？”他说话口齿分明，声音却微弱。“嗯，也许吧——也许。我本来指望——”

===Original===
“Yes, Dan?”

“I did hope I wouldn’t have to be sick very long. I’ve got so much to do. I’ve done a good deal of work, but I haven’t ever *got* anywhere with it, much. There’s a mighty big lot I’ll have to begin over, Martha. You don’t”—he paused, and laughed faintly. “You don’t—you don’t suppose God’s used me and now He’s goin’ to throw me away, do you?”

“No, no, no!” she said, making her voice cheerful. “You’ve only got to go ahead with what you began long ago.”

“No,” he said reflectively. “No; it isn’t exactly like that, Martha. Not exactly, that is. You see right now I’m a pretty complete failure—yes, I am. I’m a pretty bad failure.”

===Chinese===
“嗯，丹？”

“我本来指望不用病得太久。我要做的事太多了。活儿我干了不少，可是*干*到头来，没怎么干出个名堂。有好大一大摊子我都得从头再来，玛莎。你不会”——他顿了顿，虚弱地笑了笑。“你不会——你不会以为是上帝用完了我就要把我扔掉吧，会吗？”

“不，不，不！”她说，竭力把声音弄得欢快。“你只消接着干你老早开了头的事就是了。”

“不，”他沉思着说。“不；不完全是这样，玛莎。就是说，不完全。你要明白，眼下我败得相当彻底——是啊，我是败了。我败得很惨。”

===Original===
“You? You’re not!”

“Yes, I am,” he returned feebly. “I better face it, Martha, or I’ll never get anywhere. They’ve got Ornaby away from me—” His cough interrupted him; but he patiently let it have its way; and then, in a tone in which a wondering incredulity seemed to merge with resignation, he said, “Yes, sir; they did get Ornaby away from me!”

“But you’ll get it back, Dan?”

“Think so? Well, maybe—maybe,” he said indulgently. “But things do look like it came pretty close to a failure, Martha. It *would* have been one, too—it’d have been a bankruptcy, and I believe I just couldn’t have stood that—but, well, anyhow it wasn’t that bad, thanks to Harlan.”

Martha’s eyes widened. “Do you mean—do you mean Harlan helped you?”

===Chinese===
“你？你才不是呢！”

“不，我是，”他有气无力地答道。“我顶好面对现实，玛莎，不然我永远也成不了事。他们把奥纳比从我手里夺走了——”咳嗽打断了他；他耐着性子由着它咳完，然后用一种惊疑与认命仿佛融为一体的声气说：“是啊，先生；他们确实把奥纳比从我手里夺走了！”

“可你会把它弄回来的，对不对，丹？”

“你这么觉得？嗯，也许吧——也许，”他宽容地说。“可是眼下的样子，实在像是差一点就垮了，玛莎。那*本来*也就算垮了——那就是破产，我想我是决计受不了那个的——不过，唉，反正还没糟到那个地步，多亏了哈兰。”

玛莎的眼睛睁大了。“你是说——你是说哈兰帮了你？”

===Original===
“It was mighty good of him,” Dan said. “My friends went to him and asked him if he wouldn’t let us have some money on a second mortgage on the new house. Harlan dug out all the securities he could sell for ready cash and he brought the money to me down at Sam Kohn’s office. I must make it up to him some day. If it hadn’t been for that I’d have gone clean under!” He laughed huskily. “Everybody’d have known I was a failure for sure, if it hadn’t been for that, Martha.”

“But you’re not!” she insisted. “You mustn’t keep talking such nonsense, Dan.”

“It isn’t—it isn’t exactly nonsense.” The cough stopped him again; but he went on, while it still troubled him: “I’m a failure, Martha. I’ve been a failure in business—and a failure as a husband—and a failure as a father. George McMillan hasn’t got here with Henry yet, has he?”

“No, dear; not yet.”

===Chinese===
“他心肠真是太好了，”丹说。“我的朋友们去找他，问他肯不肯拿新房子作二次抵押，放点钱给我们。哈兰把凡是能卖成现钱的有价证券全翻了出来，把钱送到萨姆·科恩的写字间交到我手上。这情分我总有一天要报答。要不是那一下，我就整个儿沉底了！”他沙哑地笑了笑。“要不是那一下，人人就都要铁定知道我是个失败者了，玛莎。”

“可你不是！”她一口咬定。“你别再这样胡说下去了，丹。”

“这不是——这不全是胡说。”咳嗽又把他截住了；可他喘着，仍旧往下说：“我是个失败者，玛莎。做买卖我失败了——做丈夫我失败了——做父亲我也失败了。乔治·麦克米伦还没带着小亨利赶到吧，是不是？”

“没有，亲爱的；还没有。”

===Original===
Dan’s hand moved restlessly under hers, and she released it. With a visible effort he rubbed his forehead, a gesture of perplexity that hurt her and made it difficult for her to retain her appearance of cheerfulness, because this characteristic gesture brought his boyhood so vividly to her memory. “I’ve just *got* to have Henry back,” he said. “I’ve got to get him back so’s to do right by him. It isn’t—it isn’t fair to a boy, Martha.”

“What isn’t?”

“Do you remember my grandmother Savage?”

“Of course. No one could forget her, Dan.”

===Chinese===
丹的手在她手掌下面不安地动了动，她便松开了。他十分费力地揉了揉额头，这个困惑的姿势刺痛了她，叫她难以维持那副欢快的神色，因为这熟悉的姿态把他的少年时代活鲜鲜地带回了她的记忆。“我*一定*得把小亨利要回来，”他说。“我得把他要回来，好待他公平。这不——这对一个男孩子不公平，玛莎。”

“什么不公平？”

“你还记得我外婆萨维奇吗？”

“当然记得。谁也不会忘掉她，丹。”

===Original===
“No, I guess not. Well, she”—he shook his head, and half coughed, half laughed—“she was right about some things. My! but wouldn’t she be sayin’, ‘Didn’t I tell you so?’ if she knew what’s happened to my poor Henry! I’ve been a terrible failure with Henry, Martha.” He looked patiently at her as she denied this; and then he said abruptly: “Why, I’ve even been a failure with *you*, Martha!”

“That’s the absurdest thing you’ve said, dear!”

“No. I’ve been a failure as a friend, too. I let Lena fret me out of comin’ in to see you when you’d been away that long stretch. I had no business to pay any attention to her. You see—why, *you* always really liked me better than she did, Martha!”

He spoke as if it were a discovery just made; and she assented to it, taking his hand again. “Yes, Dan. I’ve always liked you better than anybody.”

“Have you?” he said inquiringly. “Well, I’m right glad to hear it. I’m right glad to hear it, Martha.”

“Yes, dear. I always have.”

===Chinese===
“是啊，我想也是。唉，她”——他摇了摇头，半是咳嗽，半是笑——“她有些事情上看得很准。我的天！她要是知道了我可怜的小亨利出了什么事，还不定要怎么念叨，‘我不是早说过了吗？’在小亨利身上，我失败得可怕极了，玛莎。”她连声否认，他只是耐心地望着她；随后他忽然说：“唉，就在*你*身上，我也失败了呢，玛莎！”

“这是你说的顶荒唐的一句话，亲爱的！”

“不。做朋友我也失败了。你离家那么久的那一阵，我由着莉娜唠叨，没进来看看你。我不该把她的话当回事。你要明白——哎呀，*你*向来是真的比莉娜更喜欢我，玛莎！”

他说这话的神气，就像刚有了一项新发现；她重新握住他的手，依顺了他。“是啊，丹。我一直最欢喜你，胜过任何人。”

“是吗？”他探问道。“那我就太高兴听了。太高兴听了，玛莎。”

“是啊，亲爱的。我一直是这样。”

===Original===
He closed his eyes, but she felt a faint pressure upon her hand from his, and sat still for a time, looking at him with fond eyes that grew frightened as the pressure upon her fingers relaxed. She was not sure, for the moment, that he was still breathing; and she looked a terrified inquiry at the grave nurse who sat on the other side of the bed. The nurse shook her head, forming with her lips the word, “Sleeping”; but Dan opened his eyes again.

“It’s curious,” he said, “the way things are. A fellow goes along, and everything seems to run all right, year after year—he can hear a little kind of grindin’ noise, maybe, sometimes, or something seems to slip, but he patches it up and doesn’t let it scare him—he keeps goin’ right along and everything seems to be workin’ about as usual—and then one thing goes wrong—and then another—and then all of a sudden the whole works pile up on top of him, and he’s down under the heap!” He took his hand again from Martha’s, and again passed it tremulously over his forehead in the old familiar gesture. “Well—maybe I could start in again if I can get over what ails me. I expect I need a good night’s rest first, though. Maybe I can sleep now.”

Martha went tiptoeing out, and through the hall to the room that had been Lena’s. Harlan was there, sitting close beside his mother. “He wants to sleep,” Martha told them, but had no sooner spoken than Dan’s renewed coughing was heard—a sound that racked the sick man’s mother. She shivered and gasped, and then, as the convulsion became fainter, went out trembling into the hall.

===Chinese===
他合上了眼睛，可她觉出他的手在她手上轻轻一按；她静静坐了一会儿，满眼爱怜地望着他，直到那按在她指上的力道松开，爱怜才变成了惊惶。一时间她拿不准他是不是还在呼吸；她朝坐在床那一边、神色肃然的护士投去惶恐的一问。护士摇了摇头，用唇形比出两个字：“睡着”；可丹又睁开了眼睛。

“说来奇怪，”他说，“事情就是这样。一个人一路走，样样都像运转得好好的，一年又一年——兴许他有时候听得见一点咯吱咯吱的磨响，或者觉出什么东西打滑，可他修补修补，不叫它吓着自己——他照旧一路走下去，样样都像照常运转——跟着，一件东西出了毛病——跟着又一件——跟着，冷不丁，整副机器一齐压到他身上，他叫压在底下那一堆里了！”他又把手从玛莎手里抽出来，又用那副熟悉的旧姿势颤巍巍地摸过额头。“唉——只要熬得过这场病，也许我还能从头来起。不过我想，我得先睡一夜好觉。也许这会儿能睡着了。”

玛莎踮着脚走出来，穿过走廊，进了那间从前是莉娜的房间。哈兰在那里，紧紧挨着他母亲坐着。“他要睡了，”玛莎对他们说，可话音未落，就听见丹又咳了起来——那声音把病人的母亲折磨得五内俱裂。她浑身一颤，倒抽一口气，随后，等那阵咳缓下来，便浑身发着抖走到走廊上去了。

===Original===
“Harlan,” Martha said, “why didn’t you tell me you tried to help Dan—at last?”

He rose, looking annoyed. “I didn’t do anything that was in the slightest degree a sacrifice,” he said. “I don’t want you to misunderstand it. I never helped him when I thought it would be thrown away, and I didn’t this time. He made over the new house to me, and I guess Lena’ll sign the deed; she’ll have to. In time it’ll probably be worth all I gave for it. I wasn’t going to see the name of Oliphant dragged through all the miserable notoriety of bankruptcy—and there was something besides.”

“Yes?” she said. “What was that?”

“Well, a pack of old money-vultures were after him, and after all Dan’s my brother.”

“Yes, he is!” Martha said. She began to cry bitterly, but silently; then suddenly she put her arms about him. “He’s still your brother, Harlan! We can say that yet;—he’s just in that room down the hall there—he’s not gone away—he’s *still* your brother, Harlan!”

But even as Martha spoke, Mrs. Oliphant, looking through the door of the sick room, cried out in terror, then rushed to her son’s bedside. Dan had unexpectedly lifted himself almost half upright; he seemed to struggle to rise; and in his eyes, wide-opened, but seeing neither his mother nor the nurse, there was a look of startled incredulity—the look of one who suddenly recognizes, to his utter astonishment, an old acquaintance long since disappeared but now abruptly returned.

A moment later the uncontrolled sobbing of his mother let Harlan know that he no longer had a brother in the room down the hall.

===Chinese===
“哈兰，”玛莎说，“你怎么没告诉我，你到底还是伸手帮了丹——到了最后？”

他站起身，神色有些不悦。“我做的事没有一丝一毫算得上牺牲，”他说。“我不要你误会了。凡是我想着会白白扔掉的时候，我从不帮他，这回也不是。他把那座新房子过了户给我，我想莉娜会在契据上签字；她不能不签。到时候这房子多半能值回我出的全部价钱。我可不愿看着奥利芬特这个姓氏在破产的种种难堪丑名里被拖来拖去——另外还有一层缘故。”

“哦？”她说。“什么缘故？”

“唉，一大群食钱的老秃鹫追着他咬，可丹到底是我哥哥。”

“是啊，他是！”玛莎说。她辛酸地哭起来，却哭不出声；随后她猛地张开两臂抱住他。“他还是你的哥哥，哈兰！这话我们眼下还说得起——他就在那头走廊底下的房间里——他没有走——他*还*是你的哥哥，哈兰！”

可是就在玛莎说话的当口，奥利芬特太太朝病房里一望，骇然惊呼起来，随即扑到儿子的床边。丹出人意外地把自己撑起了几乎半个身子；他像是挣扎着要起来；他睁得大大的眼睛里，母亲和护士他都视而不见，只有一种惊愕难信的神气——就像一个人蓦地认出一位旧相识时的神情：那人消失已久，此刻却陡然重现，叫他惊诧到了极点。

片刻之后，他母亲再也抑不住的呜咽告诉了哈兰：那头走廊底下的房间里，已经不再有他的哥哥了。

## XXXI / 三十一

===Original===
The war halted the wrecking of National Avenue, but not for long. Until the soldiers came home and the country could begin to get back into its great stride again, groups of the old, thick-walled, big-roomed houses were permitted to survive; and although it was a survival doomed, and the dignity of the dignified old things had begun to appear somewhat ridiculous, since they were smeared with the smoke-fog and begirt with automobile warehouses and salesbuildings and noisy garages and repair shops, and every other kind of shop and office, yet here and there was the semblance—or, at least, the reminder—of a fine, ample, and mannerly old street that had once been the glory of its town.

But when the great heydays came, following the collapse of the war “expansion,” and the country took up its dropped trades again, and renewed with furious and reckless energy its suppressed building, and, instead of getting back into its old great stride, set forth in a new stride gigantic beyond all its striding aforetime, then indeed the old avenue perished utterly, and nothing was left even to hint what it had been, or to tell its noble story. Old Hickory Shelby’s house was the last to go;—the stone casing of his tall front doorway was the last of all the relics. Even when the rest of the house was flat, hauled away with the fountain swan and the cast-iron fence in dumping wagons and in the trucks of junk-dealers, the doorway was allowed to remain in place above the ruins of the veranda; and for several weeks stood forth against the setting sun like a fragment on the Roman Campagna. But in time it fell, too, as the Roman fragments will.

When it was gone the old hickory stick was gone, too. He had declined to the last to be an ornament of his daughter’s fireside; and she never knew that she owed her husband’s ownership of the “new house” to her father’s insistence on a “conservative policy” for the bank of which he was one of the directors. Old Hickory’s thoughts were his own, as his ways were his own; and what he knew about himself he kept to himself, as he once or twice with a dry crackling informed his daughter.

The new house was a white house, and it remained almost white; for the smoke reached it but thinly, and in northern Ornaby, where there were other large white houses among the groves Dan Oliphant had preserved, the people struggled successfully to keep the curse under. Shrubberies lived there, not suffocated; it was a place where faces stayed clean, children throve, and lilacs bloomed in transparent air.

===Chinese===
战争叫国民大道的拆毁停了一停，可没有停多久。在大兵们还乡、国家得以重新迈开它的阔步之前，一丛丛墙厚屋阔的老宅子获准苟存；虽说这苟存是注定了的死期，那些体面老家伙的体面，也已开始显得有几分可笑了——它们满身涂抹着烟雾，被汽车栈库和展销楼、嘈杂的车库和修理铺，以及形形色色的店铺写字间团团围住——然而东一处西一处，依稀还见得出一条体面、宽绰、彬彬有礼的老街的影子——至少，还留下一点叫人想起它的东西：它曾是一座城的荣光。

可是，等到战时“扩张”崩塌之后的大盛世来临，国家重拾撂下的营生，拿出狂暴而鲁莽的气力，重振压抑已久的营造，非但没有回到旧日的阔步，反而迈出一种前所未有的大步，比它从前的任何迈步都雄伟——这时候，老街才真正死绝了，连一丝暗示它当年为何物的痕迹都没有留下，更不必说讲述它高贵的故事了。“老山核桃”谢尔比（Old Hickory Shelby）的宅子是最后拆掉的；他那高高的正门石门脸，是一切遗物中的最后一个。宅子的其余部分已经夷平，连同天鹅喷泉、铸铁栅栏一道装上倾卸大车和收旧货行商的卡车运走了，那门道却获准留在原地，立在游廊的废墟之上；一连几个星期，它背衬落日矗立着，像罗马坎帕尼亚（Roman Campagna）旷野上的一截残迹。可是到头来，它也倒了，正像罗马的残迹终究要倒的那样。

它一去，那根老山核桃木杖也随之而去了。他到死都不肯做女儿炉边的点缀；而她从来不知道，她丈夫之所以能做那座“新房子”的主人，全亏她父亲坚持要他担任董事的那家银行奉行“稳健方针”。“老山核桃”的心思是他自己的，正如他的做派是他自己的；他知道自己身上有什么，就守在自己心里——这一层，他曾有一两回干干地嘎了一声，告诉过女儿。

那座新房子是座白房子，而且到今天几乎还是白的；因为烟雾到那儿已经稀薄，而在北奥纳比——丹·奥利芬特（Dan Oliphant）保留下来的树丛之间还立着别的一些大白房子——人们成功地抗争着，把那诅咒压在了底下。在那儿，灌木丛是活着的，没有被闷死；那是一处脸庞干净、孩子茁壮、丁香在清透的空气里开花的地方。

---

===Original===
Martha drove downtown, late one afternoon of a cool day at the end of a green May, to bring her husband home from a directors’ meeting at the bank; for Harlan, in her interest, had inherited his father-in-law’s position; and, as they rolled homeward, checked now and then in the jam of traffic that filled the whole length of National Avenue, she spoke of the prevalence of “Sheridans,” those excellently serviceable cars.

“Rather!” Harlan said. “All that old rascal had to do when he got control of the Ornaby Four was to put back the old clutch and change the name. They’re all over the country. Dan would have made a great fortune if he’d lived and could have held on.”

“I don’t think he’d mind missing the fortune much,” she said. “I wish he could know how many people are riding in his cars, though. He’d like to know about that.”

They passed a gas-station, a flamboyantly painted bit of carnival, with an automobile warehouse and salesroom, and then an apartment house built round a begrimed courtyard, for its neighbours; and Harlan sighed. “It’s hard to imagine you and I once lived where these things are, isn’t it?” he said.

“Yes, some of it’s pretty ugly.”

===Chinese===
绿意将尽的五月里一个清凉的日子，天色向晚，玛莎（Martha）驾车进城，去银行接丈夫听完董事会回家；为着她的缘故，哈兰（Harlan）承袭了他岳父在银行里的位置。两口子驶上归途，一路不时被车流堵住——那车流塞满了国民大道的全程——她谈起“谢里登”（Sheridan）牌汽车如何遍地都是：那种顶好用的车。

“可不是嘛！”哈兰说。“那老滑头拿下‘奥纳比四型’之后，要办的无非是把旧离合器装回去，再换个名字。如今全国到处都是。丹要是活着，又撑得住的话，本来能发大财的。”

“我想，那笔财到不了手，他不会太往心里去，”她说。“不过，要是他能知道如今有多少人坐着他造的车，就好了。这个他会乐意知道的。”

他们驶过一座加油站——彩漆艳俗，像一小片露天游乐场——旁边一间汽车栈库和展销厅；再过去是一座公寓楼，围着一个污黑的院子而建，供它的一众邻里栖身。哈兰叹了口气。“真难想象，你我曾在这些东西丛里住过，是不是？”他说。

“是啊，有些地方真够丑的。”

===Original===
“It’s all ugly. It’s all hideous!” he said.

“No, not all.” And when they had left the avenue behind them, and reached the district of the bungalows and small wooden houses, she showed him gardens that he was forced to admit were “pretty.” But when they got beyond this, to where had been the broad stretches of woodland and meadow that Dan had planned for his “restricted residence district,” she insisted on her husband’s consent to the word “beautiful”; for the woodland was still there, so that one could hardly see the houses; and long hedges of bridal-wreath were flowering everywhere, as if snow had fallen upon the shrubberies.

“Hasn’t beauty come, Harlan?” she said.

“Oh, it’s well enough here,” he grumbled, as they swept into their own deep-shaded driveway.

===Chinese===
“整个儿都丑。整个儿都丑得吓人！”他说。

“不，不全丑。”等他们把大道抛在身后，到了平房和小木屋的地界，她指给他看一座座花园，逼得他不得不承认“好看”。可再往前，到了丹当年为他的“高端限建住宅区”（restricted residence district）规划下的那大片林地和草场的所在，她非逼丈夫点头认下“美”这个字不可；因为林地还在，房子藏在里面几乎看不见；一垄垄长长的绣线菊（bridal-wreath）处处开着花，像雪落满了灌木丛。

“美不是已经来了吗，哈兰？”她说。

“哼，这儿也还将就，”他咕哝道，这时车子已经拐进自家那条浓荫深覆的车道。

===Original===
Then they descended at white stone steps that led them up and out upon a terrace, and there they found the other member of their household sitting placidly—“to enjoy the bridal-wreath,” she said.

“Isn’t it rather chilly for you outdoors, mother?” Harlan asked; for she was now so fragile that she seemed almost transparent. “Don’t you want to go in?”

“No, not just yet,” she said. “I was just sitting here thinking how your father would have enjoyed all this. The town was pleasant when he and I were young, but of course it was never anything like this.”

“No,” Harlan said, with satire. “I should say it wasn’t!”

===Chinese===
随后他们在白石台阶前下了车，拾级而上，走到一座露台上；家中另一位成员正安然坐在那儿——说是“来赏绣线菊”，她说。

“妈，外头待着，你是不是觉得太凉了？”哈兰问；她如今衰弱得很，几乎显得透明。“不想进屋去吗？”

“不，还不想，”她说。“我正坐在这儿想，这些要是你父亲能看见，该有多高兴。我和他年轻那会儿，这城是可爱的，可自然从来没像过这样。”

“可不是，”哈兰说，带着讥诮。“我说也是万万没有！”

===Original===
“It’s a great change,” the old lady continued. “I don’t suppose my mother could have believed how beautiful it would come to be.”

“No,” Harlan said, with a short laugh. “I don’t believe she could!”

She overlooked his sarcasm, or was unaware of it, for she went on: “I don’t suppose *I* could believe how wonderful everything will be when my grandson gets to be as old as you are, Harlan.” But this thought made her wander from the subject. “I wish Lena would let him come home some day; I do want to see him;—I don’t want to go till I’ve seen him again.” Her voice became querulous, and then, with a habit she had formed in her old age, she began to talk more to herself than to her son and daughter-in-law, but for the most part in indistinct whispers. Her subject was still Henry, who had done well in the war, had been twice decorated, and now lived in Paris with his mother. The old lady murmured of him and of Lena for a little time; then fell into a reverie.

Harlan joined his wife at the terrace wall. “Well, you’ve got a supporter in mother. *She* seems to think it’s beautiful.” He pointed upward to where an opening through the foliage of tall beech trees left a vista of the sky; and there, against the evening blue, the thinning end of a plume of smoke, miles long, was visible. “Do you, really? Even that?” he asked.

===Chinese===
“真是个天大的变化，”老太太接着说。“我妈想必怎么也想不到，它会变得这么美。”

“想不到，”哈兰说，干笑一声。“我也断乎不信她能想得到！”

她没有理会儿子的讥诮，或许根本不曾觉察，只管往下说：“我想连*我*也想不到，等我的孙子到了你这岁数，哈兰，一切会美妙成什么样子。”可这个念头把她的话头引岔了。“真希望莉娜（Lena）哪天放他回来看看；我真想见他；见不着他，我可不想走。”她的声音变得絮叨委屈，随后，带着年老养成的习惯，她自言自语多过同儿子儿媳说话，而且多半是含混的低语。她的话题还是小亨利（Henry）：仗打得很好，两次授勋，如今同他母亲住在巴黎。老太太把他和莉娜絮絮念了一阵，便沉入冥想里去了。

哈兰走到露台栏杆边，同妻子并肩。“得，你有妈给你做后援。*她*好像觉得它美。”他朝天上一指——高高的山毛榉叶隙间豁开一线天穹；暮色碧空里，望得见一缕数英里长的烟羽，正在散淡的梢头。“你真觉得美？连那个也算？”他问。

===Original===
“Dan must have thought so,” she said. “I think he felt something in it that neither you nor I can understand.”

“I think maybe he did,” Harlan agreed. “Then why couldn’t he at least have lived to see the fruition of what he planted, since he loved it and it was beautiful to him? Why should he be dead and forgotten?”

“Listen!” Martha said. She was still looking up at the smoke against the sky, so far above the long masses of flowering bridal-wreath that bordered the terrace where she and her husband stood. “Listen! That murmur of the city down yonder—why, it’s almost his voice!”

===Chinese===
“丹一定是这么觉得的，”她说。“我想他在里面觉出了一种你我都不能明白的东西。”

“我想他也许是，”哈兰附和道。“那么，他既然爱它，它在他眼里又是美的，为什么他至少不能活着看见自己栽下的东西开花结果？为什么他倒该死去、被人忘掉？”

“听！”玛莎说。她还在仰望着映在天上的那缕烟——它高悬在成片开花的绣线菊之上，那些绣线菊正环绕着她与丈夫伫立的露台。“听！那远处城里的嗡嗡声——哎呀，那简直是他的声音！”
